Table 1
Foundations of Psychology: Science, Methods, and Major Perspectives
1. Q: What is psychology, and what does it study?
A: Show answer
Psychology is the scientific study of the mind, mental processes, and behaviour. It examines how individuals perceive their environment, acquire and retain information, learn, think, solve problems, experience emotions, develop throughout life, differ from one another, and interact with other people. Psychology investigates both observable behaviour and internal processes that cannot be directly observed. Its purpose is to develop systematic explanations of how people and other organisms function, why they behave in particular ways, and how psychological knowledge can be applied to improve human experience. It combines biological, cognitive, behavioural, developmental, and social perspectives to understand the complexity of mental life.

Study instruction:
- Explain the distinction between mind, mental processes, and observable behaviour.
- Identify at least five psychological processes examined by psychologists.
Key point:
- Psychology investigates both internal mental processes and external behaviour.
- Its explanations should be grounded in systematic observation and scientific reasoning.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
2. Q: Why is psychology considered a scientific discipline?
A: Show answer
Psychology is considered a science because it develops and evaluates explanations through systematic observation, measurement, hypothesis testing, evidence analysis, and logical reasoning. Psychologists formulate hypotheses, collect relevant data, examine relationships between variables, and evaluate whether their findings support proposed explanations. Research methods are designed to reduce bias, improve reliability, and establish whether results can be replicated. Although psychological phenomena are often more difficult to observe directly than physical objects, psychologists use behavioural observations, experiments, questionnaires, interviews, brain-imaging techniques, and statistical methods to investigate them. Scientific psychology also requires conclusions to be open to scrutiny and revision when new evidence becomes available.

Study instruction:
- Explain how hypothesis testing contributes to scientific knowledge.
- Distinguish scientific evidence from personal opinions and assumptions.
Key point:
- Scientific psychology relies on evidence rather than intuition alone.
- Psychological explanations must be open to testing and critical evaluation.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
3. Q: What are the principal objectives of psychology?
A: Show answer
Psychology has three closely connected objectives: describing, understanding, and predicting the psychological processes and behaviours it investigates. Description involves identifying and accurately recording what happens. Understanding involves explaining why a particular behaviour or mental process occurs and identifying the factors contributing to it. Prediction involves using established evidence and relationships to anticipate how individuals may respond under particular circumstances. Achieving these objectives enables psychologists to develop practical interventions, improve educational methods, design safer environments, and support people experiencing emotional difficulties. These objectives are interconnected because accurate description provides the foundation for explanation, while reliable explanations support prediction and practical application.

Study instruction:
- Provide one practical example of description, explanation, and prediction.
- Explain why prediction depends on sufficiently reliable evidence.
Key point:
- Psychology seeks to describe, understand, and predict behaviour and mental processes.
- Practical applications become possible when psychological explanations are sufficiently developed.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
4. Q: Why are psychological processes difficult to investigate scientifically?
A: Show answer
Psychological processes are difficult to investigate because many important mental activities cannot be observed directly. Researchers cannot ordinarily see another person's thoughts, memories, intentions, or subjective experiences. Instead, they infer these processes from observable behaviour, verbal reports, physiological responses, experimental performance, and brain activity. Another difficulty is that behaviour is multiply determined, meaning several factors may influence the same response. For example, a person's reaction to becoming lost in an unfamiliar town may depend on memory, attention, emotional state, previous experience, environmental conditions, and problem-solving ability. Researchers must therefore design studies that identify relevant influences while controlling or accounting for confounding variables.

Study instruction:
- Explain why internal mental processes require indirect measurement.
- Identify at least four factors that might influence one behavioural response.
Key point:
- Mental processes are frequently inferred rather than directly observed.
- Psychological behaviour is usually influenced by multiple interacting factors.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
5. Q: What does it mean that psychological behaviour is multiply determined?
A: Show answer
Multiply determined behaviour is behaviour influenced by several factors rather than one isolated cause. These factors may include biological characteristics, previous experiences, learning, emotions, expectations, social relationships, environmental conditions, and immediate circumstances. For example, an individual's performance during an examination may depend on knowledge, preparation, concentration, anxiety, sleep, motivation, and the examination environment. Consequently, psychologists should avoid attributing complex behaviour to a single explanation without examining alternative influences. Research must consider how variables interact and whether an apparent relationship is affected by other factors. Understanding multiple determinants helps produce more accurate explanations and more appropriate interventions.

Study instruction:
- Analyse an everyday behaviour using biological, cognitive, emotional, and environmental factors.
- Explain why identifying one associated factor does not necessarily establish the complete cause.
Key point:
- Behaviour generally results from interacting influences.
- Reliable explanations require consideration of alternative contributing factors.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
6. Q: What is the difference between observable behaviour and internal mental processes?
A: Show answer
Observable behaviour refers to actions and responses that researchers can directly record, such as speaking, walking, completing a task, or responding to a stimulus. Internal mental processes include perception, memory, attention, reasoning, intentions, and emotional experiences. These processes are not ordinarily accessible to direct external observation. Psychologists investigate them through indirect indicators, including task performance, verbal descriptions, response times, behavioural patterns, and physiological measurements. For example, memory cannot be observed directly, but researchers can assess it by asking participants to recall previously presented information. The distinction is important because an observable response does not always reveal precisely which internal process produced it.

Study instruction:
- Classify ten examples as observable behaviour or internal mental processes.
- Explain how a researcher could measure memory without observing memory itself.
Key point:
- Behaviour can be directly observed, while many mental processes must be inferred.
- Measurements must be interpreted carefully because different processes can produce similar behaviour.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
7. Q: How does evolutionary theory contribute to psychological explanations?
A: Show answer
Evolutionary theory proposes that psychological characteristics and behavioural tendencies may have developed through processes of adaptation across generations. Characteristics that helped organisms survive or reproduce could have become more common over evolutionary time. This perspective provides explanations for why certain cognitive and behavioural abilities may have developed. For example, sensitivity to moving objects may have offered advantages to ancestors who needed to detect predators or other environmental threats. Evolutionary explanations encourage psychologists to investigate the possible adaptive functions of mental processes rather than examining only how those processes operate in the present. However, an evolutionary explanation remains a hypothesis that requires appropriate evidence and should not be assumed merely because a behaviour appears useful.

Study instruction:
- Identify a psychological characteristic that could have provided an evolutionary advantage.
- Distinguish a plausible adaptive explanation from an experimentally established finding.
Key point:
- Evolutionary theory helps explain the possible origins and functions of psychological characteristics.
- Adaptive explanations require evidence rather than speculation.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
8. Q: What is introspection, and how did it influence early psychology?
A: Show answer
Introspection is the examination of one's own conscious experiences through reflection and self-observation. Early psychologists used introspection to investigate mental structures and understand the contents of consciousness. It represented an important early attempt to study psychological phenomena systematically. However, introspection has significant limitations because individuals cannot reliably observe all the processes occurring in their minds. Many mental activities operate automatically or outside conscious awareness. Personal experiences may also be influenced by memory errors, expectations, and subjective interpretations. Consequently, contemporary psychology places greater emphasis on systematic observation, behavioural evidence, experimentation, and physiological measurements rather than relying exclusively on personal reflection.

Study instruction:
- Explain how introspection was used in early psychological investigations.
- Identify three methodological limitations of introspection.
Key point:
- Introspection helped establish early investigations of consciousness.
- Subjective self-observation cannot provide a complete or consistently reliable account of mental activity.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
9. Q: How did Charles Darwin influence the development of psychology?
A: Show answer
Charles Darwin's publication of On the Origin of Species in 1859 influenced psychology by expanding attention from the structures of consciousness to their functions and possible adaptive significance. His evolutionary theory encouraged researchers to consider how psychological characteristics may have developed in response to environmental pressures. Instead of investigating only what consciousness contains, psychologists increasingly examined what mental processes accomplish and how they contribute to an organism's interaction with its environment. This perspective contributed to functional approaches in psychology and encouraged comparisons between species. Evolutionary thinking remains relevant to research examining cognition, perception, learning, emotional responses, and behavioural adaptation.

Study instruction:
- Explain the difference between studying the structure and the function of consciousness.
- Describe how evolutionary theory broadened psychological investigation.
Key point:
- Darwin's work encouraged investigation of the adaptive functions of psychological processes.
- Psychological characteristics can be studied in relation to evolutionary history.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
10. Q: What was the behaviourist movement, and how did it change psychology?
A: Show answer
Behaviourism was an influential psychological approach that emphasized observable behaviour and the scientific investigation of responses to environmental stimuli. In 1913, John Watson published a behaviourist manifesto arguing that psychology should rely on data available for public inspection rather than subjective accounts of inaccessible mental events. Early behaviourists focused on the relationship between stimuli and responses and investigated how conditioning influenced behaviour. This approach encouraged clearer behavioural definitions, systematic observation, controlled experimentation, and replicable research procedures. Although later developments restored attention to internal mental processes, behaviourism made a lasting contribution to experimental psychology and the scientific study of learning.

Study instruction:
- Explain why behaviourists emphasized observable evidence.
- Identify two contributions of behaviourism to contemporary research methods.
Key point:
- Behaviourism strengthened psychology's emphasis on observable and measurable behaviour.
- Its methods continue to influence experimental and learning research.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
11. Q: What is the stimulus-response approach in behaviourism?
A: Show answer
The stimulus-response approach examines relationships between environmental events and observable behavioural responses. A stimulus is an event or condition that can produce or influence a response, while a response is the resulting behavioural reaction. For example, a loud unexpected noise may produce an immediate startle response. Early behaviourists emphasized identifying and measuring these relationships because they could be observed experimentally. Internal processes occurring between stimulus and response were initially given relatively little attention. Later psychological research investigated these intervening processes more extensively, demonstrating that perception, attention, interpretation, memory, and expectations can influence how individuals respond to environmental events.

Study instruction:
- Construct three examples identifying the stimulus and corresponding response.
- Explain why the stimulus-response relationship may be insufficient to explain complex behaviour.
Key point:
- The approach focuses on measurable relationships between environmental stimuli and behaviour.
- Internal cognitive processes can influence the relationship between stimulus and response.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
12. Q: What are intervening variables, and why are they important in psychology?
A: Show answer
Intervening variables are theoretical or measurable processes that help explain what happens between an environmental stimulus and an observable behavioural response. They may include attention, interpretation, expectations, memory, motivation, and emotional reactions. For example, two people may receive identical criticism but respond differently because they interpret the situation differently and have different expectations or emotional experiences. Investigating intervening variables enables psychologists to move beyond simple stimulus-response explanations and develop more sophisticated accounts of behaviour. Researchers must define these variables carefully and identify suitable indicators through which their effects can be investigated.

Study instruction:
- Identify possible intervening variables in a situation involving an unexpected negative event.
- Explain how researchers could measure an internal variable indirectly.
Key point:
- Intervening variables help explain the psychological processes connecting stimuli and responses.
- Their investigation supports more comprehensive theories of behaviour.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
13. Q: What is Gestalt psychology, and what is its central principle?
A: Show answer
Gestalt psychology is an approach that investigates how people organize and experience information as meaningful wholes rather than simply as collections of separate physical elements. Its central principle is commonly expressed as the idea that the whole is greater than the sum of its parts. This means that psychological experience depends on how individual elements are organized and interpreted. For example, when two nearby lights flash successively, observers may perceive a single light moving between the two positions, even though only two stationary lights are physically present. This illustrates how the organization of sensory information influences perception and laid important foundations for contemporary cognitive psychology.

Study instruction:
- Describe an example in which the perceived whole differs from the individual physical components.
- Explain how Gestalt principles challenge purely element-based explanations of perception.
Key point:
- Perception involves active organization of information.
- The organization of individual elements can produce experiences not explained by those elements separately.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
14. Q: How did Gestalt psychology contribute to the development of cognitive psychology?
A: Show answer
Gestalt psychology demonstrated that mental organization contributes significantly to how people experience their environment. Its findings challenged the assumption that psychological experience could be explained solely through the physical characteristics of individual stimuli. This helped establish the importance of internal mental processes in explaining perception and interpretation. Cognitive psychology subsequently developed into a major field investigating how information is collected, processed, understood, stored, and used. Gestalt ideas therefore contributed to a broader recognition that the mind actively organizes information rather than passively recording environmental events.

Study instruction:
- Compare a simple stimulus-response explanation with a cognitive explanation of perception.
- Identify two mental operations involved in organizing sensory information.
Key point:
- Gestalt psychology highlighted the importance of mental organization.
- Its influence contributed to modern investigations of cognition and perception.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
15. Q: What was Sigmund Freud's contribution to psychological understanding?
A: Show answer
Sigmund Freud developed psychoanalytic theories emphasizing the influence of unconscious psychological processes and early childhood experiences on adult behaviour. His theoretical model included three structures: the id, ego, and superego. Freud proposed that unconscious wishes, desires, and conflicts could influence behaviour even when individuals were unaware of them. He interpreted phenomena such as dreams, slips of the tongue, and mannerisms as possible indicators of unconscious processes. His work encouraged psychologists to consider psychological influences outside conscious awareness. However, many classical psychoanalytic explanations have been criticized because their concepts and predictions can be difficult to test objectively or specify precisely.

Study instruction:
- Describe Freud's emphasis on unconscious processes.
- Distinguish Freud's historical contribution from the scientific limitations of classical psychoanalytic theory.
Key point:
- Freud brought unconscious influences into psychological discussion.
- The scientific testability of individual psychoanalytic explanations must be evaluated critically.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
16. Q: What are the id, ego, and superego in Freud's theoretical model?
A: Show answer
Freud proposed three theoretical psychological structures. The id represents instinctual wishes and drives and is associated with the pursuit of immediate satisfaction. The ego is concerned with negotiating between internal desires, external circumstances, and practical reality. The superego represents internalized moral standards and ideals. Freud suggested that psychological conflict could arise when these structures generate competing demands. For example, an individual may want immediate gratification, recognize practical consequences, and simultaneously feel that a particular action conflicts with personal moral standards. These concepts are components of Freud's historical theoretical framework rather than independently established anatomical structures of the brain.

Study instruction:
- Explain the theoretical function of each of the three structures.
- Apply the model to a realistic situation involving conflicting desires and responsibilities.
Key point:
- The id, ego, and superego represent different theoretical influences on behaviour.
- They should not be confused with physically identifiable brain structures.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
17. Q: Why is classical psychoanalytic theory scientifically controversial?
A: Show answer
Classical psychoanalytic theory is scientifically controversial because many of its explanations involve internal processes that are difficult to measure independently and may not generate sufficiently precise, falsifiable predictions. If a theory can explain almost any possible outcome after it occurs, it becomes difficult to determine what evidence would demonstrate that the theory is incorrect. Scientific theories should produce predictions that can be examined using objective methods. Freud's historical influence remains important, particularly regarding unconscious processes and early experiences, but individual psychoanalytic claims must be distinguished from findings supported by contemporary experimental research. Psychological explanations should be assessed according to their conceptual clarity, testability, reliability, and supporting evidence.

Study instruction:
- Explain the meaning of falsifiability in scientific research.
- Identify why vague explanations create difficulties for empirical testing.
Key point:
- Scientific explanations must be sufficiently precise to permit testing.
- Historical influence does not automatically establish scientific validity.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
18. Q: What is the cognitive revolution, and why was it important?
A: Show answer
The cognitive revolution refers to the major development of psychological research that restored systematic attention to internal mental processes, including perception, attention, memory, language, reasoning, and decision-making. Earlier behaviourist approaches had concentrated heavily on observable responses, but technological and theoretical advances made it increasingly possible to investigate the processes occurring between stimuli and responses. Researchers developed information-processing models, experimental techniques, and brain-imaging methods that enabled more detailed investigations of mental activity. The cognitive revolution also strengthened collaboration between psychology, neuroscience, computer science, linguistics, and other disciplines. It transformed psychology into a more comprehensive scientific investigation of both behaviour and mental processes.

Study instruction:
- Identify three developments that contributed to the cognitive revolution.
- Explain how cognitive psychology expanded upon earlier behaviourist approaches.
Key point:
- The cognitive revolution restored internal mental processes to the centre of psychological investigation.
- Technological advances made previously inaccessible processes increasingly measurable.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
19. Q: How have technological advances improved psychological research?
A: Show answer
Technological advances have enabled psychologists to investigate mental processes with greater precision and to examine relationships that were previously difficult to study. Electronic equipment, specialized measuring instruments, improved statistical methods, and brain-imaging technologies have expanded the available research methods. Functional magnetic resonance imaging (fMRI), for example, enables researchers to investigate changes in brain activity associated with particular tasks and conditions. Computing technology also supports the analysis of large datasets and multiple interacting variables. These developments have improved the ability to generate and test hypotheses about perception, attention, memory, thinking, and decision-making. Nevertheless, technology does not automatically guarantee accurate conclusions; research design, measurement validity, interpretation, and appropriate statistical analysis remain essential.

Study instruction:
- Identify three technologies or technical developments used in contemporary psychology.
- Explain why sophisticated equipment still requires careful interpretation.
Key point:
- Technology expands the range and precision of psychological investigation.
- Valid conclusions still depend on sound research methods and interpretation.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
20. Q: What is cognitive science, and how does it relate to psychology?
A: Show answer
Cognitive science is an interdisciplinary field that investigates the mind and its processes. It combines contributions from psychology and other disciplines to understand how information is acquired, represented, processed, stored, and used. Psychology contributes knowledge about perception, learning, memory, reasoning, attention, and behaviour. Other fields provide complementary perspectives on the brain, nervous system, language, computation, and information processing. Cognitive science is therefore broader than psychology alone. Its interdisciplinary nature enables researchers to investigate mental processes using different theoretical approaches and research techniques, creating a more comprehensive understanding of cognition.

Study instruction:
- Define cognitive science in your own words.
- Identify at least four disciplines that contribute to cognitive science.
Key point:
- Cognitive science investigates mental processes through interdisciplinary collaboration.
- Psychology is a central contributing discipline, but not the only one.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
21. Q: What is cognitive neuroscience, and what does it investigate?
A: Show answer
Cognitive neuroscience investigates the relationship between brain activity and cognitive processes. It examines how biological systems support psychological functions such as perception, action, memory, language, and selective attention. Researchers combine psychological experiments with neuroscience techniques to investigate which neural systems participate in particular cognitive activities. This approach helps explain how mental processes relate to the structure and functioning of the nervous system. Cognitive neuroscience contributes to understanding both normal psychological functioning and the consequences of neurological changes. However, identifying brain activity associated with a task does not automatically establish that the observed activity independently causes the behaviour being studied.

Study instruction:
- Explain how cognitive neuroscience differs from cognitive psychology.
- Identify three mental processes that can be investigated through cognitive neuroscience.
Key point:
- Cognitive neuroscience connects mental processes with brain functioning.
- Brain activity must be interpreted in relation to appropriate experimental evidence.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
22. Q: What is cognitive neuropsychology?
A: Show answer
Cognitive neuropsychology investigates how the structure and functioning of the brain relate to psychological processes. It often examines individuals whose cognitive abilities have changed following brain injury or neurological conditions. Patterns of preserved and impaired abilities can provide evidence about how different cognitive functions are organized and related. For example, difficulties involving memory, language, or perception may help researchers investigate the components involved in these processes. Findings from individual cases can generate hypotheses for further research, although conclusions must account for individual differences, the complexity of brain organization, and limitations in generalizing from a small number of cases.

Study instruction:
- Explain how neurological impairments can provide evidence about cognitive functioning.
- Identify one limitation of drawing general conclusions from an individual case.
Key point:
- Cognitive neuropsychology studies relationships between brain functioning and mental abilities.
- Patterns of impairment can help identify components of complex cognitive processes.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
23. Q: Why are qualitative and quantitative research methods both important in psychology?
A: Show answer
Quantitative methods produce numerical information that can be measured and statistically analysed, while qualitative methods investigate experiences, meanings, explanations, and perspectives using non-numerical information. Both approaches provide valuable but different forms of evidence. Quantitative research may establish that one treatment produces greater average improvement than another, while qualitative interviews may reveal how participants experienced each treatment and why the outcomes differed. Combining approaches can therefore provide a more comprehensive understanding of psychological phenomena. The choice of method should depend on the research question, the nature of the information required, and the limitations of available measurement techniques.

Study instruction:
- Compare quantitative and qualitative evidence using a treatment evaluation example.
- Explain when combining both approaches would improve a research investigation.
Key point:
- Quantitative methods measure patterns and relationships numerically.
- Qualitative methods help explain experiences and meanings behind observed patterns.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
24. Q: What is a laboratory experiment, and what are its strengths and limitations?
A: Show answer
A laboratory experiment tests a hypothesis under controlled conditions designed to reduce unwanted influences on the results. Researchers manipulate one or more independent variables, measure relevant outcomes, and attempt to control other factors that might influence the findings. Laboratory experiments can provide relatively strong control over research conditions, support systematic comparisons, and improve replicability. They are particularly useful when researchers need to isolate specific relationships between variables. However, artificial conditions may differ from everyday environments, meaning findings may not generalize fully to real-life behaviour. Participant awareness, demand characteristics, and limitations in participant selection may also affect the results.

Study instruction:
- Identify the independent and dependent variables in a hypothetical laboratory experiment.
- Evaluate both experimental control and ecological validity.
Key point:
- Laboratory experiments prioritize control and systematic testing.
- Strong experimental control does not automatically establish real-world generalizability.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
25. Q: What is a field experiment, and how does it differ from a laboratory experiment?
A: Show answer
A field experiment tests a hypothesis in a natural or real-world environment rather than an artificially controlled laboratory setting. It allows researchers to investigate behaviour under conditions that may more closely resemble everyday experiences. For example, a researcher might examine how changes in environmental conditions influence people's responses in an ordinary public setting. Field experiments can provide valuable information about behaviour in context, but researchers generally have less control over external influences. They may also face greater difficulties with replication, participant selection, and the isolation of individual variables. The appropriate method depends on whether experimental control or real-world relevance is more important for the research question.

Study instruction:
- Design a simple field experiment and identify possible external influences.
- Compare its advantages and disadvantages with a laboratory experiment.
Key point:
- Field experiments investigate behaviour in more natural environments.
- Their ecological relevance may be accompanied by reduced experimental control.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
26. Q: What are correlational methods, and what can they establish?
A: Show answer
Correlational methods examine the strength and direction of relationships between two or more variables. For example, researchers might investigate whether reading ability is associated with attention span. Correlation can reveal whether variables tend to change together, but it does not independently establish that one variable causes the other. An observed relationship may reflect reverse causation, the influence of an unmeasured third variable, or other underlying factors. Correlational analysis is a method of analysing relationships rather than necessarily a separate method of collecting information. Researchers should therefore interpret correlations carefully and avoid presenting associations as established causal explanations.

Study instruction:
- Identify positive, negative, and absent correlations using hypothetical examples.
- Explain why correlation does not establish causation.
Key point:
- Correlation identifies relationships between variables.
- Establishing causation requires additional evidence and appropriate research designs.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
27. Q: What are the three main types of correlation?
A: Show answer
Positive correlation occurs when two variables tend to increase or decrease together. For example, higher levels of study time may be associated with higher examination performance. Negative correlation occurs when one variable tends to increase as the other decreases. For example, greater distraction during a task may be associated with lower task accuracy. Zero or negligible correlation indicates that no meaningful linear relationship is evident between the variables. Correlation coefficients generally range from -1 to +1, with the sign indicating direction and the absolute magnitude indicating the strength of a linear relationship. A correlation coefficient does not, by itself, demonstrate causation or establish whether the relationship is practically important.

Study instruction:
- Draw a simple scatterplot illustrating each correlation type.
- Interpret hypothetical coefficients of +0.80, -0.65, and 0.05.
Key point:
- Positive and negative correlations describe different relationship directions.
- Correlation strength and causal explanation are separate issues.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
28. Q: What are the main requirements for reliable behavioural observation?
A: Show answer
Reliable behavioural observation requires researchers to define clearly what behaviour they intend to study and establish consistent procedures for recording it. Behavioural categories should be specific enough that different observers can identify the same events using similar criteria. Observation methods should also be appropriate to the research question and capture behaviour representative of the phenomenon being investigated. Researchers should consider observer bias, the influence of the observer's presence, sampling periods, recording procedures, and inter-observer agreement. When multiple observers are involved, their consistency should be evaluated rather than assumed. Careful observation supports accurate data collection and reduces the risk of drawing conclusions from incomplete or subjective impressions.

Study instruction:
- Develop an operational definition for a behaviour such as interruption or helping.
- Explain how inter-observer reliability could be assessed.
Key point:
- Behaviour must be clearly defined before it can be observed systematically.
- Consistent recording procedures improve the reliability of observations.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
29. Q: What is a case study, and when is it particularly useful?
A: Show answer
A case study is an in-depth investigation of an individual, group, or particular situation. In psychology, case studies are especially useful when researchers need detailed information about unusual experiences, neurological impairments, or changes in behaviour over time. They can help generate new hypotheses and provide insights into psychological processes that may be difficult to investigate through large experimental samples. Repeated observations under different conditions can also help researchers examine changes in an individual's functioning. However, case studies generally provide limited evidence for broad generalizations because the findings may reflect unique characteristics of the particular case. They should therefore be interpreted alongside other relevant evidence.

Study instruction:
- Identify a research question suitable for a case study.
- Explain why an individual case cannot automatically represent an entire population.
Key point:
- Case studies provide detailed information about specific cases.
- Their findings are useful for generating hypotheses but have generalizability limitations.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
30. Q: What are self-report and questionnaire methods in psychology?
A: Show answer
Self-report methods collect information from individuals about their own thoughts, feelings, experiences, attitudes, or behaviour. Questionnaires are structured instruments that ask participants to provide responses to standardized questions. These methods can collect information from large numbers of people efficiently and can investigate experiences that are not directly observable. Their reliability depends on clear question design, appropriate response options, consistent administration, and suitable sampling. However, responses may be influenced by inaccurate recall, social desirability, misunderstanding, limited self-awareness, or reluctance to disclose sensitive information. Researchers should therefore avoid assuming that reported experiences perfectly reflect actual behaviour.

Study instruction:
- Draft five questionnaire questions about a psychological topic.
- Identify possible response biases and explain how question design can reduce them.
Key point:
- Self-report provides access to individuals' reported experiences and perspectives.
- Questionnaire findings must be interpreted with awareness of response and measurement limitations.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
31. Q: What is the purpose of surveys in psychological research?
A: Show answer
Surveys collect information from individuals to investigate opinions, experiences, attitudes, behaviours, or characteristics within a population. They are particularly useful when researchers want to examine patterns across a large group rather than study only a few individuals. A properly designed survey requires a clearly defined target population, an appropriate sampling strategy, suitable questions, and consistent data collection procedures. The quality of the findings depends heavily on how representative the respondents are and whether the questions accurately measure the intended concepts. Non-response, sampling bias, ambiguous wording, and inaccurate self-reporting can all reduce the usefulness of survey findings.

Study instruction:
- Identify a target population and propose a sampling method for a psychological survey.
- Explain how a poorly representative sample could distort the conclusions.
Key point:
- Surveys support the systematic collection of information from populations.
- Sampling quality is essential for interpreting and generalizing findings.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
32. Q: How are interviews used in psychological research?
A: Show answer
Interviews collect information through direct conversations between researchers and participants. They are particularly useful for obtaining qualitative information about experiences, feelings, personal interpretations, and behavioural contexts. Structured interviews follow standardized questions, while semi-structured interviews use a planned set of topics with flexibility for follow-up questions. Less structured interviews provide greater freedom for participants to explain their experiences. Interviews can reveal information that numerical questionnaires may not capture, including how individuals understand and respond to particular situations. However, interviewer influence, participant expectations, recall limitations, and inconsistent questioning may affect the quality of the information obtained.

Study instruction:
- Develop three open-ended interview questions about a psychological experience.
- Explain how an interviewer could minimize leading questions and recording bias.
Key point:
- Interviews provide detailed qualitative information about human experience.
- Consistent, neutral questioning supports more credible findings.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
33. Q: How can psychologists combine different research methods to improve understanding?
A: Show answer
Psychologists can combine different research methods to investigate the same phenomenon from complementary perspectives. For example, researchers evaluating two treatments might use standardized questionnaires to measure changes in symptoms and semi-structured interviews to understand participants' experiences. Experiments may establish whether an intervention produces measurable differences, while interviews help explain how participants interpret those changes. Observational methods may provide additional evidence about behaviour in real-world settings. Combining methods can strengthen interpretation by revealing whether different forms of evidence support consistent conclusions. However, researchers must ensure that the methods are appropriate, that their limitations are acknowledged, and that conflicting findings are investigated rather than ignored.

Study instruction:
- Design a mixed-method study evaluating a psychological intervention.
- Explain what additional information each method would provide.
Key point:
- Different methods can provide complementary evidence.
- Combining methods improves breadth but does not eliminate methodological limitations.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
34. Q: Why must psychologists distinguish correlation from causation when interpreting research findings?
A: Show answer
Correlation indicates that variables are associated, whereas causation means that changes in one variable produce changes in another under the relevant conditions. Confusing these concepts can lead to serious errors in interpreting research findings and communicating conclusions. For example, discovering that many individuals who experienced childhood abuse later exhibit harmful behaviour does not establish that most abused children will become perpetrators. The statement concerns the proportion of perpetrators with a particular history, not the proportion of all abused individuals who subsequently engage in the behaviour. These are different conditional probabilities. Researchers must therefore examine the direction of relationships, relevant population denominators, alternative explanations, and the actual statistical evidence before drawing conclusions.

Study instruction:
- Explain the difference between P(A given B) and P(B given A).
- Identify the incorrect inference in a hypothetical research report involving two associated characteristics.
Key point:
- Association does not establish causation.
- The direction and denominator of a statistical statement must be examined carefully.

35. Q: Why are objectivity, reliability, and validity essential in psychological research?
A: Show answer
Objectivity, reliability, and validity are essential because psychological conclusions depend on the quality of the information collected and interpreted. Objectivity requires researchers to minimize personal expectations and bias when designing studies, collecting data, and evaluating findings. Reliability concerns the consistency of a measurement or research procedure. Validity concerns whether the method actually measures what it is intended to measure or supports the conclusion being drawn. A questionnaire may produce consistent results but still fail to measure the psychological characteristic it claims to assess. Similarly, accurate data can be interpreted incorrectly. These principles must therefore be considered throughout the research process, from study design to final reporting.

Study instruction:
- Explain the distinction between reliability and validity using a questionnaire example.
- Identify two practical procedures that improve objectivity.
Key point:
- Reliability concerns consistency; validity concerns appropriateness and accuracy.
- Objective data collection and interpretation reduce misleading conclusions.

- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
36. Q: What are the main branches of psychology, and what does each investigate?
A: Show answer
Psychology contains several interconnected branches, each emphasizing different aspects of psychological functioning. Abnormal psychology examines psychological dysfunctions and approaches to overcoming them. Behavioural psychology focuses on behaviour, learning, and observable evidence. Biological and comparative psychology investigate biological influences, inheritance, and psychological characteristics across species. Cognitive psychology examines information processing and the use of information. Developmental psychology studies changes throughout the lifespan. Individual-differences psychology investigates variations in characteristics such as intelligence and personality. Physiological psychology examines relationships between physiological functioning and psychological processes. Social psychology investigates behaviour and interactions within social groups. These branches overlap because psychological phenomena rarely belong to only one explanatory category.

- Classify ten practical psychological research topics according to the most relevant branch.
Key point:
- Psychology contains multiple specialized but interconnected branches.
- Different branches provide complementary explanations of human behaviour.

37. Q: What is abnormal psychology, and what does it investigate?
A: Show answer
Abnormal psychology investigates psychological dysfunctions, mental health difficulties, and approaches to understanding or overcoming psychological problems. It examines patterns of thought, emotion, and behaviour associated with psychological distress or impaired functioning. Researchers and practitioners in this field investigate the characteristics, possible contributing factors, and consequences of psychological difficulties. They may also evaluate psychological interventions designed to reduce distress and improve functioning. Abnormal psychology is closely connected with clinical practice but is not identical to psychiatry or psychotherapy. The identification and interpretation of psychological difficulties require appropriate assessment rather than assumptions based solely on unusual behaviour or individual differences.

A: Show answer
Behavioural psychology emphasizes observable behaviour, learning processes, and the environmental conditions associated with behavioural change. It investigates how experiences and interactions with the environment influence responses and learned patterns. Researchers use observation and experimentation to examine behavioural relationships and identify conditions associated with changes in behaviour. The approach has contributed to the development of learning theories and practical interventions involving reinforcement, conditioning, and behavioural modification. Although contemporary psychology recognizes the importance of internal mental processes, behavioural methods remain valuable because observable behaviour can be measured and evaluated systematically.

A: Show answer
Biological psychology investigates the biological foundations of psychological processes and behaviour, including the influence of the brain, nervous system, physiology, and inheritance. Comparative psychology examines psychological characteristics across different species to identify similarities, differences, and possible evolutionary influences. Together, these perspectives help explain how biological systems contribute to learning, perception, emotional responses, and other psychological functions. Research across species can reveal shared behavioural patterns and possible evolutionary relationships, although differences between species must be considered carefully. Biological explanations should complement rather than automatically replace cognitive, developmental, and social explanations.

Its central areas include perception, attention, learning, memory, language, reasoning, problem-solving, and decision-making. Cognitive psychologists examine how information enters the mind, how it is organized and retained, and how it influences subsequent behaviour. Research may use experiments, reaction-time measurements, questionnaires, observations, and cognitive models to investigate these processes. Cognitive psychology is important because it provides explanations for mental activities that cannot be understood adequately through observable behaviour alone.

Developmental research can investigate language acquisition, memory development, emotional regulation, social relationships, and changes in reasoning abilities. It also examines individual differences in developmental pathways. Understanding development is important because psychological processes and behaviour may change considerably with age and experience. It helps researchers and practitioners distinguish developmental changes from difficulties that require additional assessment or support.

Researchers may use standardized psychological tests, surveys, behavioural observations, and statistical analysis to examine these variations across populations. The field is important because general psychological principles do not explain every individual's characteristics or responses. Understanding individual differences supports more appropriate educational, occupational, clinical, and interpersonal applications of psychological knowledge.

It investigates how the senses, nervous system, brain, and other bodily systems influence mental activity and behaviour. It also considers how psychological states may affect physiological functioning. For example, emotional experiences may be associated with changes in heart rate, hormonal activity, and other bodily responses. Research in this field helps explain how biological processes contribute to perception, emotion, attention, and behaviour. Physiological explanations are most useful when integrated with cognitive and environmental perspectives, because biological activity occurs within broader psychological and social contexts.

Social psychology is important because human behaviour is frequently influenced by relationships and social circumstances rather than being determined solely by individual characteristics.

45. Q: Why does psychology contain multiple schools of thought rather than one universally accepted theory?
A: Show answer
Psychology contains multiple schools of thought because its subject matter involves complex mental processes that cannot be isolated easily from biological, behavioural, developmental, and social influences. Different approaches emphasize different aspects of psychological functioning. Behavioural approaches prioritize observable responses and learning, cognitive approaches investigate information processing, biological approaches examine physiological influences, and humanistic approaches emphasize subjective experiences and individual meaning. These approaches may provide complementary explanations rather than mutually exclusive answers. The absence of one universally governing paradigm reflects the complexity of the discipline, although it also creates challenges when researchers compare theories and evaluate competing explanations.

46. Q: What is the difference between psychology and psychiatry?
A: Show answer
Psychology is a broad scientific discipline concerned with mental processes, behaviour, development, and individual and social differences. Psychiatry is a medical specialty focused on the assessment and treatment of mental disorders and psychological distress. Psychiatrists are medical doctors who can prescribe medication when clinically appropriate. Psychologists are trained in psychological assessment, research, and interventions and are not generally medical doctors or authorized to prescribe medication, although professional regulations and prescribing privileges vary by jurisdiction. Both professions may work with individuals experiencing psychological difficulties, and their approaches can complement one another. The distinction is important when deciding which professional expertise is required for a particular problem.

47. Q: What is psychotherapy, and how is it related to psychology?

Psychotherapy is a broad term referring to psychological treatments intended to help individuals understand or manage emotional, behavioural, and psychological difficulties. It includes different therapeutic approaches rather than one exclusive method. These may include cognitive-behavioural, psychodynamic, humanistic, and other forms of therapy. Psychotherapy applies psychological theories and research to practical interventions designed to reduce distress and improve functioning. It should not be confused with psychology as an entire academic discipline, because psychology also includes research and applications unrelated to clinical treatment. Nor is psychotherapy synonymous with psychiatry, which is a medical specialty.

Understanding human limitations also helps organizations reduce the likelihood of errors and accidents. 53. Q: Why is critical scientific thinking important when interpreting psychological research?
A: Show answer
Critical scientific thinking is important because research findings can be misleading when information is interpreted incorrectly, important variables are ignored, or conclusions go beyond the available evidence. Researchers must examine how data were collected, whether measurements are reliable and valid, how participants were selected, whether alternative explanations exist, and whether statistical findings support the conclusions. They should also distinguish between descriptive information, associations, causal explanations, and personal interpretations. Critical thinking helps psychologists identify unsupported claims, misleading statistics, methodological weaknesses, and exaggerated conclusions. It is equally important when evaluating research published by other investigators or communicated through the media.

Study instruction:
- Evaluate a hypothetical research conclusion by identifying its evidence and assumptions.
- Identify at least four questions to ask before accepting a psychological finding.
Key point:
- Data do not speak for themselves; interpretation requires scientific judgment.
- Conclusions must remain consistent with the strength and limitations of the evidence.

54. Q: What are confounding variables, and how can researchers address them?
A: Show answer
A confounding variable is an additional factor that can influence the relationship being investigated, making it difficult to determine whether an observed effect is attributable to the variable of interest. For example, a study examining the relationship between study time and examination performance may be influenced by prior knowledge, sleep, motivation, or differences in teaching quality. Researchers can address confounding through random assignment, standardized procedures, appropriate comparison groups, careful participant selection, statistical adjustment, and measurement of relevant variables. The appropriate strategy depends on the research design. Identifying potential confounders is essential because an apparent relationship may otherwise be incorrectly interpreted as a direct causal effect.

Study instruction:
- Identify possible confounders in an investigation of stress and examination performance.
- Explain how random assignment and statistical adjustment address different methodological problems.
Key point:
- Confounding variables can distort the interpretation of observed relationships.
- Research design should identify and control or account for relevant alternative influences.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
55. Q: What happens when psychological research findings are generalized beyond the evidence?
A: Show answer
Generalizing beyond the evidence occurs when researchers or commentators apply findings to populations, situations, or causal explanations that were not adequately examined in the original study. For example, a finding about a particular group of participants may not apply to all age groups, cultures, or social environments. Similarly, a statistical association may be incorrectly presented as a universal causal relationship. Such errors can create misleading public interpretations and inappropriate practical decisions. Researchers should specify the population studied, describe methodological limitations, report relevant uncertainty, and distinguish findings from interpretations. Generalization should be supported by representative sampling, replication, and evidence from appropriate populations and settings.

Study instruction:
- Evaluate whether findings from a small university sample could be generalized to an entire national population.
- Identify the evidence required to support broader generalization.
Key point:
- Research findings apply only within the limits justified by the evidence.
- Generalization requires careful consideration of population, context, and research design.
Source links:
- Chapter 01 – Psychology, Gillian Butler
56. Q: What is the difference between basic psychological research and applied psychology?
A: Show answer
Basic psychological research aims to develop knowledge and explanations of psychological processes, often without an immediate practical application. Applied psychology uses psychological theories and research findings to address practical problems in areas such as education, healthcare, workplace safety, organizational management, and interpersonal relationships. The distinction is not absolute because basic research can eventually produce important practical benefits, while applied research can generate new theoretical knowledge. For example, investigating how memory works may initially be a basic research objective, but the resulting knowledge can inform teaching strategies and methods for supporting individuals with memory difficulties.

Study instruction:
- Classify examples of psychological research as primarily basic or applied.
- Explain how a basic research finding could eventually produce a practical intervention.
Key point:
- Basic research develops psychological knowledge.
- Applied psychology uses that knowledge to address practical problems.

57. Q: How should psychologists decide which research method is appropriate for a particular question?
A: Show answer
Researchers should select methods according to the nature of the question, the type of evidence required, the variables involved, practical constraints, and the conclusions they intend to draw. Experiments are appropriate when researchers need to investigate causal effects under controlled conditions. Correlational methods are useful when examining relationships between variables without manipulating them. Surveys and questionnaires support the collection of standardized information from larger populations, while interviews provide detailed qualitative insights. Observations investigate behaviour in context, and case studies provide intensive analysis of particular individuals or situations. Researchers should also consider feasibility, ethical requirements, measurement quality, participant characteristics, and the limitations of each method.

Study instruction:
- Select a suitable method for investigating memory, social influence, and emotional experiences.

Key point:
- Research methods must be matched to the research question.
- No single method is appropriate for every psychological investigation.

58. Q: What are the main limitations of relying on a single psychological perspective?
A: Show answer
Relying on a single psychological perspective may produce an incomplete explanation because behaviour and mental processes are influenced by multiple interacting factors. A purely biological explanation may overlook learning and social experiences, while a purely behavioural explanation may fail to account for internal interpretations, memories, and expectations. Similarly, cognitive explanations may overlook important physiological or developmental influences. Different perspectives help researchers examine the same phenomenon at different levels of explanation. Integrating perspectives can produce more comprehensive understanding, provided that their assumptions and evidence are evaluated carefully rather than combining explanations indiscriminately.

Study instruction:
- Analyse one behaviour using biological, cognitive, behavioural, and social perspectives.

- Complex psychological phenomena rarely have only one explanatory dimension.
- Integrating evidence from different perspectives can improve understanding.

59. Q: How do psychologists distinguish general psychological patterns from individual differences?
A: Show answer
General psychological patterns describe processes and characteristics shared by many people, while individual differences explain variations between individuals. Psychology investigates both because understanding common mental processes does not explain why people respond differently to similar situations. For example, most individuals use memory to retain information, but their memory performance may vary according to age, experience, attention, learning strategies, and other characteristics. Researchers examine general patterns through studies of groups and investigate individual differences through comparisons of psychological characteristics and performance. Both forms of knowledge are necessary to understand human behaviour accurately and avoid assuming that group-level findings apply identically to every individual.

Study instruction:
- Distinguish a general psychological process from an individual characteristic.
- Explain why a population average does not necessarily describe an individual accurately.
Key point:
- Psychology investigates both common psychological processes and differences between people.
- General patterns must be interpreted alongside individual variation.

Chapter 1 establishes psychology as a scientific and interdisciplinary investigation of mental processes and behaviour.
