38. Q: What does generativity versus stagnation mean during mature adulthood?
A: Show answer
Generativity versus stagnation is associated with mature adulthood, approximately ages thirty-five to sixty-four in the chapter's framework. Generativity involves contributing to the development and well-being of others and society. It may be expressed through parenting, mentoring, professional contributions, community service, teaching, leadership, or supporting younger generations. Individuals experiencing generativity often derive meaning from creating something valuable beyond their immediate personal interests. Stagnation, in contrast, involves feelings of limited contribution, lack of purpose, or insufficient engagement with others. The central challenge is to maintain meaningful involvement in activities that contribute to family, community, and society.

Study instruction:
- Identify practical examples of generativity in professional, family, and community settings.

Key point:
- Generativity involves contributing to future generations and society.
- Meaningful participation can support psychological well-being.

39. Q: What does ego integrity versus despair mean during late adulthood?
A: Show answer
Ego integrity versus despair is associated with late adulthood, approximately age sixty-five onwards in the chapter's framework. Individuals reflect on their lives, considering their accomplishments, relationships, decisions, and experiences. Ego integrity involves accepting one's life as meaningful and recognizing both successes and limitations. Despair may arise when individuals experience profound regret, dissatisfaction, or the belief that important opportunities have been permanently lost. The challenge involves developing a sense of acceptance and coherence when looking back on life. This stage highlights the importance of meaning, reflection, and psychological adjustment during older age.

Study instruction:
- Explain how life satisfaction, acceptance, regret, and personal meaning relate to Erikson's final psychosocial challenge.

Key point:
- Ego integrity involves accepting one's life as meaningful.
- Despair involves dissatisfaction and regret about one's life course.

40. Q: What are the eight stages of Erikson's psychosocial development theory?
A: Show answer
The eight stages are: (1) Early infancy: trust versus mistrust; (2) Later infancy, approximately one to three years: autonomy versus shame; (3) Early childhood, approximately three to six years: initiative versus guilt; (4) Middle childhood, approximately six to eleven years: industry versus inferiority; (5) Adolescence, approximately twelve to eighteen years: identity versus role confusion; (6) Young adulthood, approximately eighteen to thirty-five years: intimacy versus isolation; (7) Mature adulthood, approximately thirty-five to sixty-four years: generativity versus stagnation; and (8) Late adulthood, approximately sixty-five onwards: ego integrity versus despair. The stages provide a framework for understanding important psychosocial challenges across the lifespan. Their age boundaries should be treated as approximate rather than rigid biological limits.

Study instruction:
- Memorize the eight stages, their approximate ages, and their central psychosocial challenges.

Key point:
- Erikson's theory contains eight lifespan stages.
- Each stage addresses a different developmental challenge.

Source links:
- Chapter 6, Table 1: Erikson's Stages of Development
- Erikson (1968).
41. Q: Are Erikson's developmental stages fixed and universal?
A: Show answer
Erikson proposed a sequence of psychosocial stages, but subsequent research has suggested that development may be more flexible than the original framework implies. Individuals do not necessarily experience each challenge at exactly the same age or resolve it completely before encountering another. For example, some adolescents may already possess a strong sense of identity, while others continue exploring their identities well into adulthood. Social circumstances, cultural expectations, personal experiences, and individual characteristics influence the timing and expression of developmental challenges. Erikson's theory is therefore useful as a conceptual framework, but it should not be interpreted as an inflexible timetable that applies identically to every person.

Study instruction:
- Evaluate Erikson's theory by identifying both its value as a lifespan framework and its limitations concerning fixed developmental stages.

Key point:
- Psychosocial stages are useful developmental concepts, not rigid age-based rules.
- Individual and cultural differences influence developmental progression.

Source links:
- Chapter 6: Development over the Lifespan
- Erikson (1968).
42. Q: What is the cohort effect, and why is it important when studying intelligence across different age groups?
A: Show answer
The cohort effect refers to differences between generations resulting from their distinct historical, educational, social, nutritional, and environmental circumstances. It is important because studies comparing younger and older people at one point in time may incorrectly attribute differences to ageing. For example, younger generations may achieve higher intelligence-test scores because they have benefited from improved education and nutrition rather than because intelligence necessarily declines as people grow older. Researchers must therefore distinguish the effects of ageing from the effects of belonging to different generations. Failure to account for cohort effects can lead to misleading conclusions about cognitive development and ageing.

Study instruction:
- Develop an example showing how differences in education or nutrition between generations could distort an age-based intelligence comparison.

Key point:
- Cohort effects arise from differences in the experiences of generations.
- Age-group comparisons do not automatically establish the effects of ageing.

Source links:
- Chapter 6: Development over the Lifespan
43. Q: Does intelligence inevitably decline as people grow older?
A: Show answer
The chapter challenges the assumption that intelligence inevitably declines with age. Earlier studies comparing younger and older groups suggested that younger individuals had higher IQ scores. However, these comparisons did not adequately account for cohort effects. Research following the same individuals over time produced different findings, with some evidence that intelligence remained stable or increased slightly among individuals who continued using their cognitive abilities. This suggests that education, experience, mental activity, health, and social circumstances influence cognitive performance. Nevertheless, cognitive ageing is complex, and different cognitive abilities may change in different ways. Certain conditions, including dementia, can cause significant cognitive impairment and must be distinguished from normal ageing.

Study instruction:
- Compare cross-sectional and longitudinal research and explain how each method influences conclusions about intelligence and ageing.

Key point:
- Age-related cognitive changes are not uniform across individuals or abilities.
- Cohort effects can distort comparisons between younger and older groups.

Source links:
- Chapter 6: Development over the Lifespan
44. Q: Does memory inevitably deteriorate with increasing age?
A: Show answer
The chapter challenges the belief that memory inevitably deteriorates as people grow older. Memory performance depends on the type of information being remembered, attention, motivation, experience, and the demands of the task. Some studies of everyday memory have found that older people can perform as well as or slightly better than younger individuals, potentially because they are more attentive and motivated during testing. However, older adults may experience difficulties remembering names or appointments. Expectations also influence performance: individuals who believe memory deterioration is unavoidable may pay greater attention to forgetting and less attention to successful recall. Normal age-related changes should be distinguished from clinically significant cognitive impairment.

Study instruction:
- Distinguish normal memory variation, age-related changes, and cognitive impairment associated with medical conditions.

Key point:
- Memory does not decline uniformly in every individual or situation.
- Beliefs, motivation, attention, and experience influence memory performance.

Source links:
- Chapter 6: Development over the Lifespan
45. Q: How can expectations about ageing influence cognitive performance?
A: Show answer
Expectations about ageing can influence how individuals interpret and respond to changes in their cognitive abilities. If older adults believe that forgetfulness is inevitable, they may become more attentive to instances of forgetting, overlook successful recall, and reduce their effort to maintain mentally demanding activities. This can create a self-fulfilling pattern in which negative expectations contribute to poorer confidence and reduced engagement. In contrast, maintaining positive expectations and continuing to participate in challenging activities may support confidence and cognitive engagement. This does not mean that attitudes can prevent every form of cognitive decline, but it demonstrates that psychological beliefs can influence behaviour and subjective perceptions of ageing.

Study instruction:
- Explain the relationship between expectations, attention, motivation, and cognitive behaviour using a practical example.

Key point:
- Expectations can influence how people interpret cognitive changes.
- Negative stereotypes may discourage cognitive engagement.

Source links:
- Chapter 6: Development over the Lifespan
46. Q: How can people support cognitive functioning during later adulthood?
A: Show answer
Maintaining physical, mental, and social activity can support cognitive functioning during later adulthood. Mental activity provides opportunities to use and develop cognitive skills, while social engagement encourages communication, emotional interaction, and intellectual stimulation. Physical activity supports general health and may contribute indirectly to cognitive well-being. Activities such as reading, learning new skills, participating in discussions, solving problems, and maintaining relationships can help individuals remain engaged. However, these activities should not be presented as guaranteed protection against dementia or other neurological conditions. Cognitive health also depends on medical, biological, and environmental factors.

Study instruction:
- Develop a practical weekly programme combining physical activity, mental stimulation, and social participation for an older adult.

Key point:
- Continued engagement supports opportunities for cognitive activity.
- Normal ageing should be distinguished from neurological disease.

Source links:
- Chapter 6: Development over the Lifespan
47. Q: What is the difference between developmental change and individual differences within an age group?
A: Show answer
Developmental change refers to changes that occur as an individual grows older, while individual differences refer to variations between people who belong to the same age group. The distinction is important because differences between age groups may not necessarily represent the effects of ageing. For example, two adults aged sixty may have very different cognitive abilities because of differences in education, occupation, health, lifestyle, and personal experiences. Similarly, children of the same age may differ in language development, emotional regulation, and social abilities. In adulthood, variation within an age group can be greater than the average difference between age groups. Consequently, researchers should avoid assuming that age alone explains developmental outcomes.

Study instruction:
- Construct an example demonstrating how two people of the same age can have substantially different developmental outcomes.

Key point:
- Age is only one factor influencing development.
- Individual differences can be substantial within the same age group.

Source links:
- Chapter 6: Development over the Lifespan
48. Q: How do biological, social, and environmental factors combine to influence development throughout life?
A: Show answer
Development results from the combined influence of biological maturation, inherited characteristics, personal experiences, social relationships, cultural expectations, education, economic circumstances, and environmental opportunities. Biological maturation influences physical and neurological changes, while social relationships contribute to emotional and interpersonal development. Education and experience provide opportunities to develop cognitive abilities, and cultural expectations influence how individuals understand their roles and responsibilities. These influences operate throughout life and interact with one another. For example, retirement may change a person's daily activity, social interactions, and opportunities for cognitive stimulation. Development is therefore a complex process involving continuing adaptation rather than a predetermined biological sequence.

Study instruction:
- Analyse a lifespan scenario by identifying biological, psychological, social, and environmental influences.

Key point:
- Development is multidimensional and influenced by interconnected factors.
- Biological maturation and environmental experiences continue interacting throughout life.

Source links:
- Chapter 6: Development over the Lifespan
49. Q: How can developmental psychology help identify and address developmental difficulties?
A: Show answer
Developmental psychology provides information about typical developmental patterns, allowing professionals and caregivers to identify circumstances in which a person's development may require further assessment. It helps distinguish normal individual variation from possible developmental difficulties by considering behaviour, developmental history, physical and cognitive abilities, social functioning, and environmental circumstances. Where concerns arise, appropriate responses may include further assessment, educational support, health services, family guidance, rehabilitation, or specialist intervention. Early identification can help individuals access suitable opportunities and support. However, developmental expectations should be applied flexibly, and no single missed milestone should automatically be treated as a diagnosis.

Study instruction:
- Practise evaluating developmental concerns by considering multiple developmental domains and distinguishing screening concerns from formal diagnosis.

Key point:
- Developmental knowledge supports early identification and appropriate intervention.
- Individual circumstances must be considered before drawing conclusions.

Source links:
- Chapter 6: Developmental Psychology
50. Q: How can developmental psychology improve parenting and early childhood education?
A: Show answer
Developmental psychology helps parents and educators understand children's changing abilities and needs. Knowledge of developmental patterns supports age-appropriate expectations, responsive caregiving, appropriate learning opportunities, and early recognition of potential difficulties. For example, understanding the importance of language exposure encourages caregivers to communicate frequently with children. Understanding attachment encourages emotionally responsive caregiving, while knowledge of cognitive development helps educators provide suitable learning activities. Developmental psychology also supports the design of interventions for children experiencing social disadvantage or inadequate developmental opportunities. Its practical value lies in translating research findings into environments that support healthy physical, cognitive, emotional, and social development.

Study instruction:
- Design an age-appropriate learning and caregiving activity and explain which developmental principles support it.

Key point:
- Developmental knowledge improves caregiving and educational decisions.
- Supportive environments can help maximize developmental potential.

- Chapter 6: Developmental Psychology
51. Q: What is the difference between early attachment and later social relationships?
A: Show answer
Early attachment refers to an enduring emotional bond between a young child and a caregiver who provides security and comfort. Later social relationships include friendships, romantic partnerships, professional relationships, and other interpersonal connections developed throughout life. Early attachment experiences may influence expectations about trust, emotional availability, and interpersonal security. However, later relationships are also shaped by temperament, personal experiences, social learning, culture, and subsequent supportive or adverse events. Consequently, early attachment can provide useful information about developmental experiences but should not be treated as a permanent blueprint that determines every future relationship.

Study instruction:
- Explain how early relationship experiences may influence later social behaviour while identifying factors that can modify those influences.

Key point:
- Early attachment can influence later interpersonal expectations.
- Later experiences continue shaping relationships and social development.

52. Q: How would you evaluate the claim that childhood determines a person's adult personality?
A: Show answer
The claim is an oversimplification. Childhood experiences can influence emotional development, attachment, social behaviour, learning, and later psychological functioning. However, adult personality and behaviour are also shaped by inherited characteristics, adolescent experiences, adult relationships, education, work, cultural circumstances, and major life events. Evidence involving children who experienced early deprivation demonstrates that adverse childhood conditions can have lasting effects, but also shows that later supportive environments can improve outcomes. Similarly, Erikson's lifespan framework emphasizes that psychological development continues throughout adulthood. A more accurate conclusion is that childhood is important but does not independently determine every aspect of adult personality or life outcomes.

Study instruction:
- Develop an argument that considers both the lasting influence of childhood and the capacity for later development and adaptation.

Key point:
- Childhood experiences are influential but not exclusively determinative.
- Development continues through later relationships and life experiences.

Source links:
- Chapter 6: Effects of Early Experience
- Chapter 6: Development over the Lifespan
- Erikson (1968).
53. Q: How would you distinguish a developmental delay from normal developmental variation?
A: Show answer
Normal developmental variation involves differences in the timing or sequence of developmental achievements that do not necessarily indicate a disorder. A developmental delay involves slower-than-expected progress in one or more developmental domains and may require further assessment. The distinction should consider the individual's age, developmental history, overall functioning, health, environmental opportunities, and consistency of observed difficulties. For example, differences in the timing of walking or language acquisition should be assessed in the context of other developmental abilities rather than judged through a single milestone. Persistent difficulties across several settings or developmental domains may justify professional evaluation. A formal diagnosis requires appropriate clinical assessment rather than informal comparison with other children.

Study instruction:
- Evaluate developmental scenarios using multiple indicators rather than relying on one age-based milestone.

Key point:
- Developmental variation is not automatically a disorder.
- Persistent concerns should be evaluated through appropriate professional assessment.

Source links:
- Chapter 6: Developmental Psychology
- Chapter 6: Effects of Early Experience
54. Q: What would you recommend if a child experienced prolonged neglect or inadequate social interaction?
A: Show answer
The first priority would be to ensure the child's immediate safety and establish a stable, supportive caregiving environment. The child's physical health, nutrition, developmental abilities, emotional functioning, and social needs should then be assessed by appropriately qualified professionals. An individualized support plan may include consistent caregiving, opportunities for safe social interaction, language stimulation, educational support, and psychological or developmental intervention where necessary. Progress should be monitored across several developmental domains, and the plan should be adjusted according to the child's response. The approach should recognize that recovery may take time and that the effects of deprivation can differ substantially between individuals.

Study instruction:
- Develop an intervention plan that identifies immediate protective measures, assessment requirements, responsible professionals, support activities, and progress indicators.

Key point:
- Safety and stable caregiving are immediate priorities.
- Recovery requires individualized, coordinated, and continuing support.

Source links:

55. Q: How would you assess whether an intervention has improved a child's development?
A: Show answer
Intervention effectiveness should be evaluated by comparing the child's functioning before and after support is provided. Assessment should cover relevant domains, including physical development, language, cognitive abilities, emotional regulation, social interaction, and everyday independence. Evidence may come from structured developmental assessments, observations by caregivers and educators, professional evaluations, and documented progress towards individual goals. It is important to distinguish genuine developmental improvements from temporary changes or differences in assessment conditions. Reviews should also identify continuing difficulties, new needs, and factors that may be preventing progress. An effective intervention is responsive to evidence and adjusted when the child's circumstances or developmental needs change.

Study instruction:
- Prepare a developmental monitoring framework with baseline observations, measurable objectives, evidence sources, review dates, and criteria for adjusting support.

Key point:
- Intervention should be monitored against identifiable developmental outcomes.
- Progress evaluation supports continuous improvement in care and intervention.

Source links:
- Chapter 6: Developmental Psychology

56. Q: What are the main lessons of developmental psychology for understanding human development across the lifespan?
A: Show answer
The central lesson is that human development is a complex, continuous, and multidimensional process influenced by biological maturation, genetic characteristics, environmental conditions, relationships, and personal experiences. Development generally follows recognizable patterns, but individuals differ in their timing and experiences. Early experiences, particularly caregiving, social interaction, and opportunities for learning, can have important consequences, although later supportive experiences may reduce some adverse effects. Brain development continues into adulthood, and psychosocial development remains relevant throughout life. Cognitive ageing is also more variable than traditional assumptions suggest. Understanding these principles helps researchers, parents, educators, and professionals identify developmental needs, improve support, and recognize the potential for adaptation and growth at different stages of life.

Study instruction:
- Review the chapter by connecting nature and nurture, developmental stages, brain maturation, attachment, early experience, Erikson's theory, and cognitive ageing into one integrated explanation.

Key point:
- Development is lifelong, multidimensional, and influenced by interacting factors.
- Early experiences matter, but later opportunities and supportive environments also shape outcomes.

Table 7
Individual Differences: Psychological Assessment, Intelligence and Personality
1. Q: Can we categorize people, and what determines individual differences?
A: Show answer
Individual differences are the psychological, cognitive, emotional, behavioural, and personality characteristics that distinguish one person from another. Psychology examines whether these differences can be identified, measured, categorized, and explained through inherited characteristics, environmental influences, personal experiences, and interactions between individuals and their surroundings. Two particularly important areas are intelligence and personality. Psychological assessment provides methods for examining these differences, but categorization should not be confused with defining an individual's complete identity. People are complex, and their behaviour can change according to circumstances, learning, motivation, and development.

Study instruction:
- Explain the meaning of individual differences and distinguish intelligence from personality.
- Review the respective contributions of biological, environmental, and situational influences.
Key point:
- Individual differences arises through multiple interacting influences.
- Psychological categories describe patterns rather than completely defining individuals.
Source links:
- American Psychological Association – Personality
2. Q: What are individual differences in psychology?
A: Show answer
Individual differences refer to relatively consistent variations between people in their abilities, interests, attitudes, emotional responses, motivations, temperament, and behaviour. These differences can be examined through psychological observation, standardized testing, interviews, questionnaires, and behavioural research. Some characteristics are relatively stable, while others change with age, education, experience, health, and social circumstances. Studying individual differences helps psychologists understand why people respond differently to similar situations and why individuals achieve different outcomes despite having comparable opportunities.

Study instruction:
- Identify examples of cognitive, emotional, behavioural, and personality differences.
- Distinguish relatively stable characteristics from temporary behavioural states.
Key point:
- Individual differences include both abilities and patterns of behaviour.
- Stability does not mean that characteristics are completely unchangeable.
Source links:
- APA – Personality
3. Q: Why do psychologists study individual differences?
A: Show answer
Psychologists study individual differences to understand variations in human behaviour, identify psychological strengths and difficulties, explain differences in achievement, and improve predictions about how people may respond to particular circumstances. Practical applications include educational assessment, career guidance, clinical evaluation, personnel selection, and psychological research. Understanding individual differences also helps professionals avoid assuming that everyone learns, thinks, communicates, or reacts in the same way. However, predictions must account for the limitations of assessment instruments and the influence of environmental conditions.

Study instruction:
- Examine applications of individual-difference research in education, employment, and clinical psychology.
- Explain why individual assessment is more informative than relying exclusively on general assumptions.
Key point:
- Individual-difference research supports more informed assessment and decision-making.
- Predictions should consider personal and situational circumstances.
Source links:
- APA – Testing and Assessment
4. Q: What is psychological measurement?
A: Show answer
Psychological measurement is the systematic assessment of psychological characteristics using instruments designed to quantify abilities, attributes, attitudes, behaviours, or other psychological constructs. Examples include intelligence tests, personality inventories, aptitude assessments, achievement tests, and behavioural rating scales. Unlike physical measurements, psychological characteristics such as intelligence and personality cannot generally be observed or measured directly. They are inferred from responses, performance, and behavioural indicators. Consequently, the quality of psychological measurement depends on the instrument's reliability, validity, standardization, and suitability for the intended population and purpose.

Study instruction:
- Compare direct physical measurement with the indirect measurement of psychological constructs.
- Identify the main characteristics of a scientifically defensible psychological instrument.
Key point:
- Psychological measurement uses observable indicators to assess underlying constructs.
- The interpretation of a score depends on the quality of the measurement instrument.
Source links:
- NCBI – Overview of Psychological Testing
5. Q: What is a psychometric test?
A: Show answer
A psychometric test is a standardized psychological measurement instrument designed to assess characteristics such as intelligence, personality, aptitude, memory, reasoning, or other abilities. It typically involves structured questions, tasks, statements, or activities that generate scores for interpretation. A good psychometric instrument requires appropriate evidence of reliability and validity. It should also have suitable administration procedures, scoring methods, and reference information. Psychometric tests can support professional judgments, but their results should not automatically be treated as definitive descriptions of a person's capabilities or characteristics.

Study instruction:
- Identify the purpose, structure, scoring method, and intended population of different psychometric instruments.
- Review the evidence supporting the interpretation of test scores.
Key point:
- Psychometric instruments measure defined psychological characteristics.
- Their results require appropriate professional interpretation.
Source links:
- NCBI – Overview of Psychological Testing
6. Q: What is reliability in psychological testing?
A: Show answer
Reliability refers to the consistency and precision of measurement. A reliable test produces sufficiently consistent results when administered under comparable conditions, assuming the characteristic being measured has not meaningfully changed. Common forms include test-retest reliability, internal consistency, and inter-rater reliability. For example, if a person completes the same ability assessment on two occasions under comparable conditions, substantially different scores may indicate measurement instability. However, reliability does not establish that the test measures the intended characteristic. It establishes consistency, not necessarily accuracy.

Study instruction:
- Distinguish test-retest reliability, internal consistency, and inter-rater reliability.
- Consider factors that can produce inconsistent test results.
Key point:
- Reliability concerns the consistency of measurement.
- A reliable instrument is not automatically a valid instrument.
Source links:
- NCBI – Psychometrics and Reliability
- APA – Ethical Principles, Section 9: Assessment
7. Q: What is validity in psychological testing?
A: Show answer
Validity concerns whether the evidence supports the interpretation and use of test scores for a particular purpose. A reading assessment, for example, should provide meaningful evidence about reading ability rather than primarily measuring unrelated characteristics such as general background knowledge. Validity is not simply a permanent property of a test. It depends on the intended interpretation, population, setting, and decision being made. Evidence may include test content, internal structure, relationships with relevant external measures, and the consequences of test use.

Study instruction:
- Review content, criterion-related, and construct-related evidence of validity.
- Explain why a test validated for one population or purpose may not be appropriate for another.
Key point:
- Validity concerns the defensibility of score interpretations and uses.
- A test must be appropriate for its specific assessment purpose.
Source links:
- APA – Guidelines for Psychological Assessment and Evaluation
8. Q: What is the difference between reliability and validity?
A: Show answer
Reliability concerns consistency, whereas validity concerns whether the interpretation and use of the results are justified. A weighing scale that consistently adds five kilograms produces reliable but inaccurate measurements. Similarly, a psychological test may consistently measure reading speed when it is intended to measure reading comprehension. The results could be reliable but insufficiently valid for the intended purpose. Reliability is generally necessary for meaningful validity, because highly inconsistent measurements cannot support dependable conclusions. Nevertheless, reliability alone cannot establish validity.

Study instruction:
- Construct examples of reliable but invalid measurements.
- Explain why consistent results are insufficient without appropriate validity evidence.
Key point:
- Reliability means consistency.
- Validity means defensible interpretation and use.
Source links:
- NCBI – Overview of Psychological Testing
- APA – Psychological Assessment Guidelines
9. Q: What does standardization mean in psychological testing?
A: Show answer
Standardization means establishing consistent procedures for administering, scoring, and interpreting an assessment. It also commonly involves collecting results from an appropriate reference sample to establish norms. Standardized administration helps ensure that differences in scores are more likely to reflect differences between individuals rather than differences in instructions, testing environments, timing, or scoring procedures. Standardization requires clear administration manuals, appropriate training, consistent scoring rules, and suitable reference populations.

Study instruction:
- Review the standardization process from test development to norm establishment.
- Identify how inconsistent administration can distort comparisons between individuals.
Key point:
- Standardization promotes comparability between test results.
- Consistent procedures reduce avoidable measurement variation.
Source links:
- APA – Testing and Assessment
10. Q: What are norms in psychological testing?
A: Show answer
Norms are statistical reference values obtained from a defined group of people who complete an assessment under standardized conditions. They help interpret an individual's score relative to the performance of an appropriate comparison population. For example, a score may be described as average, above average, or below average based on the distribution of scores in the relevant norm group. Norms must be suitable for the individual's age, language, cultural background, and other relevant characteristics. Outdated or inappropriate norms can produce misleading interpretations.

Study instruction:
- Explain how norm groups are selected and why their representativeness matters.
- Distinguish a raw score from a norm-referenced score.
Key point:
- Norms provide a reference for interpreting individual performance.
- A score's meaning depends partly on the comparison group.
Source links:
- APA – Psychological Assessment Guidelines
11. Q: How are intelligence test scores interpreted?
A: Show answer
Intelligence test scores are interpreted by comparing an individual's performance with the relevant standardized reference distribution. Many conventional IQ scales use a mean of 100 and a standard deviation of 15, although specific instruments and scoring systems can differ. On such a scale, approximately 68% of the reference population falls within one standard deviation of the mean, and approximately 95% falls within two standard deviations under a normal-distribution model. Interpretation should consider measurement error, confidence intervals, the assessment's purpose, and relevant personal and cultural circumstances.

Study instruction:
- Practice interpreting standardized scores and percentile ranks.
- Understand why a numerical score should be interpreted with its confidence interval and assessment context.
Key point:
- IQ scores express relative performance on a particular standardized assessment.
- They are estimates, not complete measures of human potential.
Source links:
- NCBI – Overview of Psychological Testing
12. Q: Why must psychological testing conditions be standardized?
A: Show answer
Testing conditions can influence performance independently of the characteristic being assessed. Poor lighting, excessive noise, unclear instructions, interruptions, fatigue, anxiety, and unsuitable testing environments may affect concentration and responses. Standardized conditions seek to minimize these influences so that scores can be compared more fairly. Test administrators should follow the instrument's instructions, provide appropriate accommodations where necessary, document deviations, and consider whether testing conditions affected the results.

Study instruction:
- Identify environmental, procedural, and personal factors that can influence assessment performance.
- Practice documenting departures from standardized administration procedures.
Key point:
- Testing conditions can influence observed scores.
- Administration procedures should be consistent and appropriately documented.
Source links:
- APA – Ethical Principles, Section 9: Assessment
13. Q: What are the main limitations of psychological tests?
A: Show answer
Psychological tests cannot capture every dimension of human ability, personality, motivation, or behaviour. Their results may be affected by measurement error, cultural differences, language proficiency, educational opportunity, test familiarity, temporary emotional states, and the quality of the instrument. Some tests measure only a narrow selection of abilities, while others depend on self-reported information that may be inaccurate or deliberately distorted. Another limitation is that test results may predict general tendencies without reliably predicting behaviour in every individual situation. Consequently, psychological assessment should integrate multiple relevant sources of information rather than rely exclusively on one score.

Study instruction:
- Evaluate the strengths and limitations of different assessment methods.
- Explain why psychological test results should be interpreted alongside other relevant evidence.
Key point:
- No single psychological test captures the whole person.
- Multiple sources of evidence improve assessment quality.
Source links:
- APA – Professional Practice Guidelines for Psychological Evaluations
14. Q: What is intelligence in psychology?
A: Show answer
Intelligence is a complex psychological construct generally associated with learning, reasoning, problem-solving, understanding, adaptation, and the application of knowledge to unfamiliar situations. It may involve abstract reasoning, logical thinking, memory, verbal comprehension, numerical ability, creativity, and the capacity to learn from experience. Psychologists have disagreed about whether intelligence represents one general mental ability or several distinct but related abilities. This disagreement influences how intelligence is defined and measured. Intelligence should therefore be understood as a multidimensional and theoretically complex construct rather than an unquestionable single characteristic.

Study instruction:
- Compare definitions of intelligence based on general ability and multiple abilities.
- Identify cognitive activities commonly associated with intelligent behaviour.
Key point:
- Intelligence involves multiple cognitive capabilities.
- Its precise definition remains dependent on theoretical and measurement approaches.
Source links:
- Annual Review of Psychology – Intelligence
15. Q: What is the general intelligence factor (g)?
A: Show answer
The general intelligence factor, commonly called g, refers to a general cognitive ability proposed to contribute to performance across different intellectual tasks. The concept emerged from observations that individuals who perform well in one cognitive assessment often perform relatively well in others. Under this approach, intelligence includes a broad general factor alongside more specific abilities. The general factor helps explain correlations between different cognitive tasks, but it does not mean that every intellectual ability is identical or that individuals have uniform strengths across all domains.

Study instruction:
- Examine the distinction between general cognitive ability and specific cognitive abilities.
- Consider why performance on different mental tasks may be correlated.
Key point:
- The g factor represents shared variance across cognitive abilities.
- Specific abilities can still differ considerably between individuals.
Source links:
- Annual Review of Psychology – Intelligence
16. Q: What are specific intelligence factors?
A: Show answer
Specific intelligence factors refer to particular cognitive abilities that contribute to performance in defined domains. Examples include numerical reasoning, verbal comprehension, memory, spatial reasoning, perceptual speed, and problem-solving. Individuals may demonstrate considerable strengths in one area while experiencing difficulties in another. This helps explain why general intelligence scores cannot fully describe an individual's cognitive profile. Assessing specific abilities can provide additional information for educational planning, career guidance, and identifying particular learning strengths or difficulties.

Study instruction:
- Identify different cognitive abilities and the tasks used to assess them.
- Compare a general intelligence score with a profile of specific abilities.
Key point:
- Cognitive abilities are not necessarily uniform across domains.
- Specific assessments can reveal strengths hidden by an overall score.
Source links:
- Annual Review of Psychology – Intelligence
17. Q: What are the main types of tasks used in intelligence tests?
A: Show answer
Intelligence tests commonly contain several subtests designed to assess different cognitive functions. These may include general knowledge questions, arithmetic problems, vocabulary definitions, verbal similarities, memory tasks, picture sequencing, pattern recognition, block arrangement, and spatial puzzles. Some assessments emphasize processing speed, while others emphasize reasoning, comprehension, or working memory. The combination of subtests depends on the theoretical framework and purpose of the instrument. Performance across the different subtests can provide an overall estimate and a more detailed cognitive profile.

Study instruction:
- Match common intelligence-test activities with the cognitive abilities they assess.
- Explain why a test may contain multiple subscales instead of one type of question.
Key point:
- Intelligence tests assess several related cognitive functions.
- Subtest profiles can provide information beyond the overall score.
Source links:
- NCBI – Overview of Psychological Testing
18. Q: What is the difference between chronological age and mental age?
A: Show answer
Chronological age refers to the actual time elapsed since a person was born. Mental age is a historical concept used to describe a child's performance relative to the average performance of children at different developmental ages. For example, a seven-year-old who performs at the average level of a ten-year-old on a particular developmental assessment might historically be described as having a mental age of ten. However, mental age is not a complete description of intellectual functioning, and contemporary psychological assessment relies on more sophisticated age-standardized scores and developmental interpretations.

Study instruction:
- Distinguish chronological age from historical mental-age interpretations.
- Review how age-related norms are used in contemporary cognitive assessments.
Key point:
- Chronological age measures actual age.
- Mental age is a historical comparison of developmental performance.
Source links:
- APA – Testing and Assessment
19. Q: What is fluid intelligence?
A: Show answer
Fluid intelligence refers to the ability to reason, recognize patterns, solve unfamiliar problems, and identify relationships without depending primarily on previously acquired knowledge. It is commonly associated with abstract reasoning and the ability to respond to novel situations. Examples include solving unfamiliar mathematical patterns, identifying logical relationships, and completing non-verbal reasoning problems. Fluid intelligence is influenced by biological development and other factors, and its pattern of development differs from that of accumulated knowledge.

Study instruction:
- Practice identifying tasks that require novel reasoning rather than memorized knowledge.
- Compare fluid intelligence with crystallized intelligence.
Key point:
- Fluid intelligence concerns reasoning in unfamiliar situations.
- It is not equivalent to the totality of intellectual ability.
Source links:
- Annual Review of Psychology – Intelligence
20. Q: What is crystallized intelligence?
A: Show answer
Crystallized intelligence refers to accumulated knowledge, vocabulary, learned skills, and practical understanding developed through education and experience. It reflects the ability to apply previously acquired information to problems and everyday situations. Examples include understanding complex vocabulary, using professional knowledge, applying mathematical principles learned through education, and explaining established concepts. Crystallized intelligence can continue developing through learning and experience, although the pattern varies according to age, health, opportunity, and other influences.

Study instruction:
- Identify examples of acquired knowledge and learned problem-solving skills.
- Examine how education and experience contribute to crystallized abilities.
Key point:
- Crystallized intelligence reflects accumulated knowledge and experience.
- Learning opportunities influence its development.
Source links:
- Annual Review of Psychology – Intelligence
21. Q: What is the difference between fluid and crystallized intelligence?
A: Show answer
Fluid intelligence emphasizes reasoning and solving unfamiliar problems, whereas crystallized intelligence emphasizes knowledge and skills accumulated through experience. For example, discovering the rule behind a previously unseen pattern primarily involves fluid reasoning, while explaining the meaning of a technical term learned through professional education relies more heavily on crystallized knowledge. The two abilities are related rather than completely independent. Both contribute to intelligent performance, and their development may follow different patterns across the lifespan.

Study instruction:
- Classify everyday cognitive tasks as primarily fluid or crystallized.
- Explain why both abilities contribute to effective problem-solving.
Key point:
- Fluid intelligence emphasizes novel reasoning.
- Crystallized intelligence emphasizes accumulated knowledge.
Source links:
- Annual Review of Psychology – Intelligence
22. Q: What are savant abilities, and what do they demonstrate about intelligence?
A: Show answer
Savant abilities refer to exceptional performance in a particular domain alongside significant limitations in other areas of functioning in some individuals. Examples can include extraordinary calendar calculation, musical ability, detailed memory, or exceptional numerical performance. These cases demonstrate that cognitive abilities can be unevenly distributed and that exceptional performance in one area does not necessarily indicate equally exceptional performance in every other area. They also raise important questions about how intelligence should be conceptualized and measured.

Study instruction:
- Examine examples of uneven cognitive profiles.
- Explain why a single overall score may not capture exceptional domain-specific abilities.
Key point:
- Cognitive abilities can differ substantially within the same individual.
- Exceptional performance in one domain does not establish general superiority.
Source links:
- Annual Review of Psychology – Intelligence
23. Q: How do genetic factors influence intelligence?
A: Show answer
Genetic differences contribute to variation in measured cognitive abilities across populations. Research involving twins, families, and adopted individuals has identified substantial genetic influences on intelligence-related characteristics. However, heritability is a population-level statistical estimate, not a measurement of how genetically determined an individual person's intelligence is. Genetic influences operate within environments and interact with nutrition, education, health, family circumstances, and social opportunities. Consequently, genetic influence does not imply that intelligence is fixed or that environmental interventions are ineffective.

Study instruction:
- Distinguish heritability from genetic determination.
- Review how family, twin, and adoption studies contribute to research on intelligence.
Key point:
- Genetic differences contribute to individual variation in cognitive abilities.
- Heritability does not establish an individual's fixed intellectual potential.
Source links:
- Annual Review of Psychology – Intelligence
24. Q: How does the environment influence intelligence?
A: Show answer
Environmental influences affect cognitive development through nutrition, physical health, education, family interaction, socioeconomic circumstances, exposure to learning opportunities, and the quality of the surrounding environment. Children who receive appropriate educational stimulation, adequate nutrition, and supportive learning conditions may develop cognitive skills more effectively than those experiencing significant deprivation. Environmental influences can also affect test performance through stress, limited educational exposure, language differences, and familiarity with assessment formats. Genetic and environmental influences operate together rather than as completely separate explanations.

Study instruction:
- Identify environmental factors that can support or restrict cognitive development.
- Explain how environmental circumstances can influence both actual ability and measured performance.
Key point:
- Cognitive development is influenced by environmental conditions.
- Improving learning opportunities can support intellectual development.
Source links:
- Annual Review of Psychology – Intelligence
25. Q: How do twin studies help researchers understand the influence of genes and the environment on intelligence?
A: Show answer
Twin studies compare similarities in measured characteristics between identical twins, who share almost all their inherited genetic variation, and fraternal twins, who share approximately half their segregating genetic variation on average. Researchers examine whether greater genetic similarity is associated with greater similarity in cognitive scores. Studies of identical twins raised apart can provide additional information about environmental influences. However, these studies require careful interpretation because twins may share prenatal conditions, similar family circumstances, and other environmental experiences. Findings describe population-level patterns and do not determine an individual's cognitive potential.

Study instruction:
- Compare identical and fraternal twin research designs.
- Identify environmental and methodological limitations of twin studies.
Key point:
- Twin studies help estimate genetic and environmental contributions.
- Shared environments complicate the separation of genetic and environmental effects.
Source links:
- Annual Review of Psychology – Intelligence
26. Q: How do adoption studies help distinguish genetic and environmental influences on intelligence?
A: Show answer
Adoption studies examine cognitive similarities between adopted children and their biological and adoptive relatives. Greater similarity with biological relatives may provide evidence of genetic contributions, while similarities with adoptive relatives may indicate environmental influences. However, interpretation is complicated by selective placement, prenatal influences, differences in socioeconomic circumstances, and the possibility that adoptive families provide environments related to the characteristics of biological parents. Adoption studies therefore contribute to a broader body of evidence rather than independently proving the cause of individual differences.

Study instruction:
- Compare adoption studies with twin studies.
- Explain why prenatal and post-adoption environmental influences must be considered.
Key point:
- Adoption research helps investigate genetic and environmental contributions.
- No single research design completely separates nature from nurture.
Source links:
- Annual Review of Psychology – Intelligence
27. Q: What is the nature-versus-nurture debate?
A: Show answer
The nature-versus-nurture debate concerns the relative contributions of inherited biological characteristics and environmental experiences to human development and individual differences. Nature includes genetic influences and biological predispositions, while nurture includes education, upbringing, nutrition, social relationships, culture, and life experiences. Contemporary psychological explanations generally recognize that these influences interact. For example, inherited characteristics may influence how a person responds to educational opportunities, while environmental experiences can influence how biological potential is expressed. The debate is therefore better understood as an investigation of interacting influences rather than a choice between two exclusive explanations.

Study instruction:
- Identify examples of genetic and environmental influences on intelligence and personality.
- Explain how gene-environment interaction differs from treating nature and nurture as competing explanations.
Key point:
- Human development reflects interacting biological and environmental influences.
- Neither inherited characteristics nor experience alone explains all individual differences.
Source links:
- Annual Review of Psychology – Intelligence
- APA – Personality
28. Q: Can intelligence be increased through environmental interventions?
A: Show answer
Environmental interventions can improve cognitive development, learning outcomes, and performance on particular intellectual tasks. Examples include high-quality education, adequate nutrition, healthcare, enriched learning environments, and sustained parental or caregiver support. However, improvements in performance on trained tasks do not automatically demonstrate a general increase in intelligence. Some interventions produce benefits that are specific to the skills being practiced, while others may generate broader developmental improvements. The duration, intensity, timing, and quality of an intervention influence its outcomes.

Study instruction:
- Distinguish improvements in specific cognitive skills from generalized changes in intelligence.
- Examine evidence on education, nutrition, and early-childhood interventions.
Key point:
- Environmental interventions can support cognitive development.
- Improvements in one task should not automatically be interpreted as generalized intelligence enhancement.
Source links:
- Annual Review of Psychology – Intelligence
29. Q: What is the difference between brain training and broader cognitive development?
A: Show answer
Brain training generally involves repeated practice of selected cognitive tasks, such as memory exercises, attention activities, or reasoning puzzles. Performance often improves on the trained activities because individuals become more familiar with the tasks and develop relevant strategies. However, improvement on trained activities does not necessarily transfer to unrelated cognitive abilities or produce lasting changes in general intelligence. Broader cognitive development involves multiple influences, including education, health, social interaction, and sustained learning experiences. It should therefore be evaluated through a wider range of outcomes rather than performance on a single training exercise.

Study instruction:
- Distinguish task-specific practice effects from transfer to other cognitive abilities.
- Evaluate claims about brain-training programmes using evidence of lasting and generalized benefits.
Key point:
- Practice improves performance on practiced activities.
- Generalized cognitive benefits require separate supporting evidence.
Source links:
- Annual Review of Psychology – Intelligence
30. Q: What was the Head Start programme, and what was its purpose?
A: Show answer
Head Start is a United States early-childhood development programme established in 1965 to support children from disadvantaged backgrounds. Its approach combines educational services with health, nutrition, family support, and other developmental services. The programme recognizes that children's development is influenced by multiple interconnected circumstances rather than education alone. Evaluations have examined its effects on school readiness, educational achievement, health, and longer-term outcomes. Its historical experience also illustrates why comprehensive and sustained support may be more effective than short interventions in addressing long-standing developmental disadvantages.

Study instruction:
- Examine the programme's historical objectives and integrated service model.
- Distinguish educational outcomes from broader developmental and family outcomes.
Key point:
- Head Start addresses multiple dimensions of early-childhood development.
- Educational support is more effective when considered alongside other developmental needs.
Source links:
- U.S. Administration for Children and Families – Head Start
31. Q: Why may the effects of early-childhood educational interventions diminish over time?
A: Show answer
Early educational interventions may initially improve children's cognitive performance and school readiness, but some differences can diminish if subsequent educational environments do not provide continued support. Children who receive a high-quality early intervention may later enter schools with substantially different teaching quality, resources, and learning opportunities. Consequently, initial gains may not be maintained equally across participants. This does not necessarily mean the intervention was ineffective. It indicates that sustained educational support and continuity are important for maintaining developmental progress.

Study instruction:
- Examine how the quality of subsequent schooling influences early intervention outcomes.
- Distinguish short-term improvements from sustained long-term effects.
Key point:
- Early intervention benefits can depend on later environmental conditions.
- Continued support helps sustain developmental improvements.
Source links:
- U.S. Administration for Children and Families – Head Start
32. Q: What is personality in psychology?
A: Show answer
Personality refers to the relatively enduring organization of characteristics that influence how individuals think, feel, behave, and interact with the world. It includes traits, attitudes, interests, motives, values, emotional patterns, and interpersonal tendencies. Personality is not directly observable as a single physical characteristic. Instead, psychologists infer it from recurring patterns of behaviour, self-reported responses, and other relevant evidence. Although personality often demonstrates relative stability, it can develop and change through maturation, relationships, learning, and significant life experiences.

Study instruction:
- Identify the main components commonly included in psychological definitions of personality.
- Explain the difference between a personality trait and a temporary emotional state.
Key point:
- Personality describes relatively enduring psychological characteristics.
- It is influenced by biological, social, and experiential factors.
Source links:
- APA – Personality
33. Q: Why is personality difficult to define and measure?
A: Show answer
Personality is difficult to define because psychologists have proposed different explanations of the characteristics and processes that determine behaviour. Some theories emphasize stable traits, others emphasize learning, unconscious motives, cognitive processes, or situational influences. Personality cannot be measured directly in the same way as height or weight. Instead, it is inferred from observations, questionnaires, interviews, and recurring behavioural patterns. Measurement is further complicated by self-report inaccuracies, social desirability, changing circumstances, cultural differences, and the possibility that individuals behave differently across situations.

Study instruction:
- Compare the different theoretical explanations of personality.
- Identify the strengths and weaknesses of questionnaires and behavioural observations.
Key point:
- Personality is an inferred psychological construct.
- Different theoretical assumptions produce different measurement approaches.
Source links:
- APA – Personality
34. Q: What are the main approaches to theories of personality?
A: Show answer
Major personality approaches include categorical or type theories, trait theories, behaviourist theories, cognitive theories, psychodynamic theories, individual or humanistic approaches, situational approaches, and interactionist approaches. Each offers a different explanation of why people demonstrate relatively consistent or differing patterns of behaviour. Type approaches classify people into broad categories, while trait approaches describe individuals according to the degree to which they possess particular characteristics. Behaviourist theories emphasize learning and reinforcement, cognitive theories emphasize thought processes, psychodynamic theories emphasize unconscious processes, and interactionist approaches combine individual characteristics with situational influences.

Study instruction:
- Create a comparison of the principal personality theories and their explanations of behaviour.
- Identify the main assumptions underlying each approach.
Key point:
- Personality theories explain individual differences from different theoretical perspectives.
- No single approach explains every aspect of personality and behaviour.
Source links:
- APA – Personality
35. Q: What is the categorical or type approach to personality?
A: Show answer
The categorical approach classifies individuals into broad personality types based on shared characteristics. Examples include introversion and extraversion or particular traditional type classifications. It emphasizes similarities between individuals who belong to the same category and differences between categories. This approach can make personality concepts easier to communicate, but it may oversimplify individual differences because people do not always fit neatly into distinct categories. Many characteristics are better represented as continuous dimensions on which individuals can score at different levels.

Study instruction:
- Examine examples of personality classifications.
- Explain the limitations of assigning individuals to rigid personality categories.
Key point:
- Type theories organize individuals into broad categories.
- Continuous dimensions often describe personality differences more accurately.
Source links:
- APA – Personality
36. Q: What is the trait approach to personality?
A: Show answer
The trait approach describes personality through relatively enduring characteristics that vary in degree between individuals. Rather than assigning everyone to a distinct category, it considers how strongly a person demonstrates particular traits. Examples include conscientiousness, sociability, emotional stability, openness, and agreeableness. An individual may score relatively high on some traits and relatively low on others. Trait approaches are useful for describing personality variation and studying relationships between characteristics and behavioural outcomes. However, traits represent tendencies rather than guarantees of how someone will behave in every situation.

Study instruction:
- Identify examples of personality traits and describe how individuals can differ in their levels.
- Distinguish a trait description from a categorical personality label.
Key point:
- Trait theories describe personality as varying along dimensions.
- Traits indicate tendencies rather than fixed behavioural outcomes.
Source links:
- APA – Personality
37. Q: What is the behaviourist approach to personality?
A: Show answer
The behaviourist approach emphasizes the influence of learning history, conditioning, and reinforcement on behaviour. From this perspective, recurring behavioural patterns develop partly because particular responses have previously been rewarded, encouraged, or reinforced. For example, a child who receives positive reinforcement for sharing may become more likely to demonstrate cooperative behaviour. Behaviourist explanations emphasize observable behaviour and environmental influences rather than relying primarily on internal personality structures. However, behaviour alone cannot explain every individual difference because cognitive processes, biological characteristics, and personal interpretations also influence responses.

Study instruction:
- Review classical conditioning, operant conditioning, and reinforcement.
- Identify how environmental consequences can influence recurring behaviour.
Key point:
- Behaviourist theories emphasize learning and reinforcement.
- Behaviour is influenced by more than reinforcement alone.
Source links:
- APA – Personality
38. Q: What is the cognitive approach to personality?
A: Show answer
The cognitive approach emphasizes the role of beliefs, expectations, interpretations, memory, and thought processes in determining behaviour. People can respond differently to the same situation because they interpret it differently. For example, one individual may interpret a colleague's silence as criticism, while another may assume the colleague is distracted. These interpretations influence emotional reactions and subsequent behaviour. Cognitive approaches therefore examine how individuals process information and how their beliefs and expectations contribute to relatively consistent patterns of responding.

Study instruction:
- Identify how beliefs and interpretations influence emotional and behavioural responses.
- Analyze examples in which two people interpret the same event differently.
Key point:
- Behaviour is influenced by how individuals interpret situations.
- Similar external circumstances can produce different psychological responses.
Source links:
- APA – Personality
39. Q: What is the psychodynamic approach to personality?
A: Show answer
The psychodynamic approach, historically associated with Sigmund Freud, emphasizes unconscious motives, internal psychological conflicts, and the influence of early experiences on personality development. Freud proposed three interacting personality structures: the id, ego, and superego. The id represents instinctual drives, the ego mediates between internal desires and external reality, and the superego represents internalized moral standards. Psychodynamic theories suggest that behaviour may be influenced by psychological processes outside conscious awareness. Although Freud's specific theoretical claims have been substantially debated, psychodynamic perspectives remain historically important in the development of personality psychology.

Study instruction:
- Explain Freud's concepts of the id, ego, and superego.
- Distinguish historical psychodynamic theory from contemporary evidence-based personality research.
Key point:
- Psychodynamic theory emphasizes unconscious processes and internal conflicts.
- Freud's original model should be understood as a historical theoretical framework, not an established complete explanation of personality.
Source links:
- APA – Personality
40. Q: What is the individual or humanistic approach to personality?
A: Show answer
The individual or humanistic approach emphasizes the person as a whole, including subjective experiences, personal meaning, autonomy, values, and higher psychological motives. Rather than dividing personality exclusively into separate traits or mechanisms, this perspective considers the individual's complete experience and capacity for development. Humanistic theories are associated with concepts such as personal growth, self-understanding, self-actualization, and the pursuit of meaningful goals. This approach highlights aspects of personality that may be overlooked when psychological assessment focuses only on standardized scores or observable behaviour.

Study instruction:
- Review the concepts of personal growth, self-concept, autonomy, and self-actualization.
- Compare humanistic explanations with trait and behaviourist approaches.
Key point:
- Humanistic approaches emphasize personal experience and development.
- Understanding personality requires considering the individual as a whole.
Source links:
- APA – Personality
41. Q: What is the situational approach to personality?
A: Show answer
The situational approach emphasizes the influence of external circumstances on behaviour. It suggests that behaviour may vary substantially according to environmental demands, social expectations, and the consequences associated with particular actions. For example, an individual may behave confidently among close friends but remain reserved in a formal professional meeting. Situational approaches challenge the assumption that personality traits determine behaviour consistently across all circumstances. However, explaining behaviour entirely through external circumstances also overlooks individual differences in interpretation, motivation, and emotional response.

Study instruction:
- Analyze how changes in social context can influence the same person's behaviour.
- Compare situational explanations with trait-based explanations.
Key point:
- Situations influence how personality is expressed.
- Individuals do not necessarily respond identically to similar circumstances.
Source links:
- PubMed – Person-Situation Interactions in Personality
42. Q: What is the interactionist approach to personality?
A: Show answer
The interactionist approach proposes that behaviour results from the interaction between individual characteristics and situational circumstances. People possess relatively enduring tendencies, but the expression of those tendencies depends partly on the demands and interpretation of the situation. For example, an individual who generally prefers quiet environments may confidently deliver a presentation when sufficiently prepared and motivated. This approach offers a more comprehensive explanation than assuming that either personality traits or external circumstances independently determine behaviour.

Study instruction:
- Examine examples where individual traits and situational demands jointly influence behaviour.
- Practice explaining behaviour through both personal and environmental factors.
Key point:
- Behaviour reflects interactions between individuals and situations.
- Personality tendencies do not produce identical responses in every context.
Source links:
- PubMed – Person-Situation Interactions in Personality
43. Q: What is the fundamental attribution error?
A: Show answer
The fundamental attribution error refers to the tendency to overemphasize personal characteristics when explaining another person's behaviour while underestimating situational influences. For example, someone who arrives late may be judged as irresponsible without considering traffic conditions, transport disruptions, or an unexpected emergency. This cognitive tendency can produce inaccurate judgments about personality and character. It is particularly relevant when interpreting individual differences because observed behaviour does not necessarily reveal a person's enduring characteristics.

Study instruction:
- Identify examples in which situational circumstances are overlooked when judging another person.
- Practice separating observed behaviour from assumptions about underlying personality.
Key point:
- Behaviour should not automatically be attributed to personality.
- Situational explanations should be considered before making judgments.
Source links:
- APA – Personality
44. Q: What is Eysenck's theory of personality?
A: Show answer
Hans Eysenck developed a personality framework combining elements of type and trait approaches. His original model emphasized two major dimensions: introversion-extraversion and emotional stability-neuroticism. Individuals could be positioned at different points along these dimensions rather than being assigned exclusively to rigid categories. Eysenck used statistical methods to identify patterns among personality characteristics and proposed biological explanations for some individual differences. His theory helped demonstrate how broad personality dimensions can be constructed from relationships among narrower traits. Later personality research developed additional models and dimensions, so Eysenck's framework should be understood as one influential historical approach rather than the only accepted personality model.

Study instruction:
- Explain the two principal dimensions in Eysenck's original framework.
- Compare Eysenck's model with modern multidimensional personality approaches.
Key point:
- Eysenck combined type and trait perspectives.
- Personality dimensions represent continuous individual differences.
Source links:
- APA – Personality
45. Q: What is introversion?
A: Show answer
Introversion is a personality characteristic generally associated with greater preference for less externally stimulating environments, reflection, and more selective social interaction. Introverted individuals may prefer smaller social gatherings, independent activities, or time to process information internally. Introversion does not necessarily mean shyness, social anxiety, poor communication, or an inability to lead. An introverted person can perform confidently in public settings, establish strong relationships, and demonstrate effective leadership. The extent of introversion varies between individuals and is best understood as part of a continuous personality dimension.

Study instruction:
- Distinguish introversion from shyness and social anxiety.
- Identify how introverted tendencies may be expressed differently across situations.
Key point:
- Introversion concerns tendencies and preferences, not an inability to socialize.
- Introverted individuals can demonstrate strong social and professional capabilities.
Source links:
- APA – Personality
46. Q: What is extraversion?
A: Show answer
Extraversion is a personality dimension commonly associated with sociability, assertiveness, positive emotional expression, activity, and responsiveness to rewarding social experiences. Individuals with higher extraversion may actively seek social interaction, enjoy group activities, and demonstrate greater outward expressiveness. However, extraversion does not guarantee confidence, happiness, leadership ability, or superior performance. Its expression depends on the person and the circumstances. Extraversion is generally measured as a continuous characteristic rather than a strict division between two fundamentally different types of people.

Study instruction:
- Identify behavioural characteristics commonly associated with extraversion.
- Distinguish social energy and assertiveness from competence and achievement.
Key point:
- Extraversion describes a broad personality tendency.
- High extraversion does not automatically imply superior performance or well-being.
Source links:
- APA – Personality
47. Q: What is neuroticism in personality psychology?
A: Show answer
Neuroticism is a personality dimension associated with differences in emotional stability and susceptibility to negative emotional experiences. Individuals with higher neuroticism may experience anxiety, worry, emotional sensitivity, and stronger reactions to stressful circumstances. Those with lower neuroticism generally demonstrate greater emotional stability across situations. Neuroticism is a personality trait dimension and should not automatically be interpreted as a clinical mental disorder. Its relationship with behaviour depends on other personal characteristics, environmental circumstances, and available coping resources.

Study instruction:
- Distinguish neuroticism as a personality trait from clinical psychiatric diagnoses.
- Examine how emotional stability can influence responses to stress.
Key point:
- Neuroticism concerns individual differences in emotional reactivity.
- A personality trait is not equivalent to a clinical diagnosis.
Source links:
- APA – Personality
48. Q: What is ambiversion?
A: Show answer
Ambiversion describes individuals whose characteristics fall between the more introverted and more extraverted ends of the personality continuum. An ambivert may enjoy social interaction in some circumstances while preferring independent activities in others. Their behaviour may depend on energy levels, social relationships, familiarity, task requirements, and environmental stimulation. Ambiversion illustrates why dividing everyone into two rigid personality categories can be misleading. Many people demonstrate a mixture of characteristics rather than consistently displaying one extreme.

Study instruction:
- Examine introversion and extraversion as continuous rather than strictly categorical dimensions.
- Identify circumstances in which the same person may demonstrate both tendencies.
Key point:
- Many individuals fall between the extremes of personality dimensions.
- Personality expression varies across circumstances.
Source links:
- APA – Personality
49. Q: What are the biological explanations of introversion and extraversion?
A: Show answer
Biological explanations examine whether individual differences in personality are associated with variations in brain functioning, neural responses, inherited characteristics, and physiological processes. Eysenck proposed that differences in baseline arousal contributed to introversion and extraversion. Subsequent research has examined associations between personality characteristics and neural systems involved in reward sensitivity, attention, emotional processing, and approach behaviour. However, identifying a neural association does not establish that a single brain mechanism completely causes a personality characteristic. Personality reflects complex interactions between biological processes and experience.

Study instruction:
- Review the historical arousal explanation proposed by Eysenck.
- Distinguish neural correlations from demonstrated causal mechanisms.
Key point:
- Biological processes contribute to personality differences.
- No single brain characteristic fully explains an individual's personality.
Source links:
- APA – Personality
50. Q: What is the Big Five personality model?
A: Show answer
The Big Five is a widely used framework for describing personality through five broad dimensions: openness to experience, conscientiousness, extraversion, agreeableness, and neuroticism.
