20. Q: How can an ordinary group member become a leader through organizational circumstances?
A: Show answer
Research on group communication demonstrates that leadership can emerge when an individual is placed in a central position within a communication network. In the experiment described in the chapter, group members were required to communicate through one central person. That person gradually began performing leadership functions, including sending more messages, solving problems faster, and making fewer errors. The individual also became more satisfied with the group's efforts and was recognized by other members as a leader. This suggests that leadership can develop through structural opportunities, communication responsibilities, and group expectations rather than requiring an individual to possess an established reputation as a natural leader.

Study instruction:
- "Explain how communication structures and task responsibilities can create opportunities for leadership to emerge."
Key point:
- Leadership can emerge from a person's position within a group.
- Group structure can influence who becomes recognized as a leader.
21. Q: What is conformity, and how does it influence individual attitudes and behaviour?
A: Show answer
Conformity is the process of matching one's attitudes, beliefs, or behaviour to perceived group norms. Individuals may conform because they want to be accepted, avoid criticism, obtain accurate information, or maintain harmonious relationships. Conformity affects many areas of life, including clothing, communication, professional conduct, social expectations, and everyday cooperation. It can produce beneficial outcomes, such as helping vulnerable people and respecting orderly queues. However, it can also discourage independent judgment and encourage individuals to support incorrect decisions when they believe that the majority expects agreement.

Study instruction:
- "Identify examples of beneficial and harmful conformity and explain the motivations underlying each."
Key point:
- Conformity involves adjusting behaviour or attitudes to perceived group norms.
- Conformity can support cooperation but can also suppress independent judgment.
22. Q: Why do individuals conform to the opinions and behaviour of a majority?
A: Show answer
Individuals conform for several reasons. They may believe that the majority possesses more accurate information, particularly when a situation is ambiguous. This is an informational motivation because the individual uses other people's responses to help interpret reality. Alternatively, individuals may conform because they fear social rejection, criticism, or embarrassment. This is a normative motivation because the individual wants to maintain acceptance within the group. Conformity can also develop through repeated exposure to shared expectations, which gradually become accepted social norms. The strength of these influences depends on the importance of group membership, the individual's confidence, and the difficulty of the situation.

Study instruction:
- "Distinguish informational influence from normative influence and identify examples of each."
Key point:
- People may conform because they seek accurate information.
- People may also conform because they seek social acceptance.
23. Q: How did Solomon Asch investigate conformity through his line-judgment experiment?
A: Show answer
Solomon Asch investigated conformity by asking participants to identify which of three comparison lines had the same length as a reference line. Participants were placed in groups containing confederates who had been instructed to give incorrect answers on selected trials. The genuine participant therefore faced a situation in which the majority repeatedly provided an answer that was visibly incorrect. The experiment demonstrated that many individuals conformed to the majority at least some of the time. On average, participants gave the incorrect majority answer approximately one-third of the time on the critical trials. The experiment showed that group pressure can influence even straightforward perceptual judgments.

Study instruction:
- "Review the experimental arrangement, the role of confederates, the incorrect majority responses, and the principal findings."
Key point:
- Asch demonstrated that majority pressure can influence individual judgments.
- Individuals may conform even when the correct answer appears clear.
24. Q: What were the principal findings of Asch's conformity experiment?
A: Show answer
Asch found that most participants conformed to the incorrect majority at least once. On average, participants selected the wrong answer approximately one-third of the time on critical trials. The findings demonstrated that individuals can publicly agree with a group even when their own judgment suggests that the group is incorrect. The experiment also showed that conformity is not inevitable: many participants resisted majority pressure and maintained their independent judgments. The results established a foundation for examining the psychological conditions that increase or reduce conformity.

Study instruction:
- "Memorize the principal findings and distinguish the proportion of participants who conformed at least once from the average rate of conformity across critical trials."
Key point:
- Majority influence produced substantial but not universal conformity.
- Independent judgment remained possible despite group pressure.
25. Q: How does ambiguity influence conformity?
A: Show answer
Ambiguity increases conformity because individuals become less certain about the accuracy of their own judgments. When the correct answer is difficult to identify, other people's responses may appear to provide useful information. Individuals may therefore rely more heavily on the majority as a source of guidance. In contrast, when the correct answer is obvious, people may feel more confident resisting social pressure. This demonstrates that conformity depends partly on the individual's perception of the task and the level of uncertainty involved.

Study instruction:
- "Compare conformity in situations involving obvious answers with conformity in situations involving uncertain or ambiguous judgments."
Key point:
- Greater uncertainty can increase dependence on group judgments.
- Confidence in one's own assessment can reduce conformity.
26. Q: How does group unanimity affect conformity?
A: Show answer
Group unanimity increases conformity because individuals may feel that disagreeing with a completely united majority is socially difficult. In Asch's experiments, the presence of even one person who disagreed with the majority reduced conformity. The dissenting individual demonstrated that unanimous agreement was not necessary and gave the genuine participant greater confidence to express an independent judgment. This finding highlights the importance of independent opinions and constructive disagreement in group decision-making. A single dissenting voice can weaken the perceived pressure to agree with everyone else.

Study instruction:
- "Explain how a single dissenting group member can influence the willingness of others to disagree with the majority."
Key point:
- Unanimity increases the pressure to conform.
- One dissenting opinion can substantially reduce conformity.
27. Q: What is compliance as a form of conformity?
A: Show answer
Compliance occurs when an individual publicly follows a group's expectations or agrees with the majority while privately maintaining a different opinion. The individual changes observable behaviour without necessarily accepting the group's underlying beliefs. In Asch's experiments, some participants who gave the incorrect majority answer knew that their answer was wrong. They nevertheless conformed because they did not want to disagree, feared social disapproval, or wanted to avoid disrupting the experiment. Compliance is therefore primarily associated with outward agreement rather than genuine internal acceptance.

Study instruction:
- "Identify situations in which an individual publicly agrees with a group but privately disagrees."
Key point:
- Compliance involves outward conformity without necessarily changing private beliefs.
- Fear of disapproval can motivate public agreement.
28. Q: What are identification and internalization as deeper forms of conformity?
A: Show answer
Identification and internalization involve deeper forms of accepting the attitudes, beliefs, or behaviour of other people or groups. Identification occurs when individuals adopt attitudes or behaviours because they value their relationship with a particular person or group. Internalization involves accepting beliefs or values as personally valid and incorporating them into one's own thinking. These forms of conformity differ from superficial compliance because the influence may continue even when the original social pressure is absent. The chapter associates deeper acceptance with sources perceived as credible or expert, particularly when situations are difficult or urgent.

Study instruction:
- "Compare compliance, identification, and internalization by examining whether the individual changes public behaviour, personal beliefs, or both."
Key point:
- Compliance can occur without private agreement.
- Identification and internalization involve deeper acceptance of social influence.
29. Q: How does the credibility or expertise of a person influence conformity?
A: Show answer
People are more likely to accept the opinions of individuals whom they perceive as knowledgeable, experienced, or credible. When a situation is difficult or urgent, individuals may rely on experts because they believe that those people possess information that they lack. For example, during a medical emergency, a person may follow a doctor's instructions because the doctor is perceived to have relevant professional expertise. However, perceived credibility does not guarantee that the advice is correct. Individuals and organizations should distinguish genuine expertise from authority based solely on reputation, position, or confidence.

Study instruction:
- "Evaluate how expertise, credibility, and perceived authority influence acceptance of advice in difficult situations."
Key point:
- Perceived expertise can increase social influence.
- Advice should still be assessed for accuracy and relevance.
30. Q: What other factors influence the degree of conformity?
A: Show answer
Conformity is influenced by gender, culture, situational circumstances, task difficulty, observation, the importance of accuracy, familiarity with group members, and the value individuals place on other people's opinions. Individuals may conform more strongly when they are uncertain, when they are being observed, or when maintaining group relationships is particularly important. Cultural expectations can also influence the extent to which group agreement and individual independence are emphasized. These factors interact, meaning that conformity cannot be explained through one universal cause.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare examples showing how cultural expectations, group relationships, task difficulty, and observation can influence conformity."
Key point:
- Conformity is influenced by multiple personal and situational factors.
- The importance of group membership affects willingness to agree.
31. Q: What is obedience, and how does it differ from conformity?
A: Show answer
Obedience is compliance with instructions or demands issued by an authority figure. It involves responding to a person's perceived position of authority rather than simply matching the behaviour or opinions of a majority. Conformity is primarily associated with group norms and social expectations, whereas obedience involves a direct instruction from someone regarded as having authority. Both processes can influence behaviour without genuine personal agreement. For example, an employee may follow a manager's instruction because of the manager's formal position, while another employee may adopt the behaviour of colleagues because it has become the group's accepted norm.

Study instruction:
- "Compare conformity and obedience, focusing on the source of influence, the role of authority, and the individual's motivation."
Key point:
- Conformity involves adjustment to group norms.
- Obedience involves responding to an authority figure's instructions.
32. Q: Why did psychologists begin investigating obedience in the context of war and atrocities?
A: Show answer
Psychological investigations of obedience were motivated partly by atrocities committed during wars, including the Holocaust and the killing of Vietnamese civilians at My Lai. These events raised questions about how ordinary individuals could participate in severe wrongdoing while claiming that they were following orders. Researchers wanted to understand whether authority, social pressure, situational circumstances, and psychological mechanisms could influence people to perform actions that conflicted with their personal moral standards. The investigations demonstrated the importance of examining individual responsibility, institutional authority, and the conditions under which people obey harmful instructions.

Study instruction:
- "Review the historical motivation for obedience research and explain why following orders does not eliminate individual responsibility."
Key point:
- Historical atrocities stimulated research into obedience.
- Authority and institutional pressure can influence morally significant behaviour.
33. Q: What was Stanley Milgram's experiment on obedience designed to investigate?
A: Show answer
Milgram's experiment investigated how far ordinary people would go in following instructions from an authority figure, even when those instructions appeared to cause harm to another person. Participants were told that they were participating in a study of memory and were assigned the role of teacher. They were instructed to administer increasingly powerful electric shocks to a learner whenever the learner answered incorrectly. The experimenter remained present and instructed participants to continue when they expressed concerns. The learner was actually an actor and did not receive the shocks. The study demonstrated the substantial influence that perceived authority can exert over individual behaviour.

Study instruction:
- "Review the experimental roles, the apparent electric-shock procedure, the experimenter's instructions, and the actual purpose of the study."
Key point:
- Milgram investigated obedience to authority.
- The apparent harmful consequences created a conflict between obedience and personal conscience.
34. Q: How was Milgram's electric-shock experiment organized?
A: Show answer
Participants were told that they would act as teachers in a memory experiment. The learner, who was actually an actor, was strapped into an electrically wired chair and given an apparent sample shock of 45 volts. Participants then sat in front of a generator containing 30 switches ranging from 15 volts to 450 volts. They were instructed to increase the shock level whenever the learner made a mistake. The learner was trained to make errors and respond with increasingly distressed reactions as the supposed shock intensity increased. The experimenter remained in the room and instructed participants to continue. The arrangement created an apparent conflict between following experimental instructions and responding to another person's suffering.

Study instruction:
- "Reconstruct the sequence of the experiment and distinguish the participant's understanding of events from what was actually happening."
Key point:
- Participants believed that they were administering real electric shocks.
- The learner's reactions were simulated by an actor.
35. Q: What were the main findings of Milgram's obedience experiment?
A: Show answer
The chapter reports that approximately 65 per cent of participants continued to the highest level of the shock generator, marked 450 volts. None stopped before the 300-volt level, when the learner began kicking the adjoining wall. Many participants objected and questioned whether they should continue, but the experimenter instructed them to proceed. The findings demonstrated that ordinary individuals could continue performing apparently harmful actions when instructed by someone perceived to possess legitimate authority. The results do not establish that everyone will obey authority or that obedience is inevitable; rather, they demonstrate the strength of situational influences under the experimental conditions.

Study instruction:
- "Remember the reported 65 per cent result, the 300-volt point, and the significance of the experimenter's presence."
Key point:
- Approximately 65 per cent reached the maximum shock level in the reported experiment.
- Perceived authority can produce substantial obedience despite personal discomfort.
36. Q: How did the experimenter's physical presence influence obedience in Milgram's research?
A: Show answer
The experimenter's physical presence substantially increased obedience. When instructions were delivered by telephone rather than directly in the participant's presence, obedience declined from approximately 65 per cent to 21 per cent. Some participants also cheated by administering weaker shocks than instructed. This finding suggests that immediate supervision and the visible presence of an authority figure can strengthen compliance. Physical presence may increase the perceived importance of instructions, create social pressure, and make refusal more uncomfortable. It also demonstrates that obedience is influenced by the circumstances in which authority is exercised.

Study instruction:
- "Compare the reported obedience rates under direct supervision and telephone instructions and explain the effect of reduced physical presence."
Key point:
- Direct physical presence increased obedience in the reported experiment.
- Reducing immediate supervision made resistance more likely.
37. Q: Why can individuals find it difficult to disobey an authority figure?
A: Show answer
Individuals may find disobedience difficult because of social expectations, politeness conventions, fear of criticism, concern about consequences, and reluctance to challenge someone regarded as an authority. In Milgram's experiment, participants had already accepted the role of teacher and entered into the experimental procedure. Refusing to continue may have required admitting that the experimenter's instructions were wrong and that the apparent harm to the learner was unacceptable. These psychological pressures can make it progressively harder to withdraw from an activity. In real organizations, hierarchical relationships and fear of punishment may further discourage individuals from questioning inappropriate instructions.

Study instruction:
- "Identify psychological and institutional barriers that can prevent individuals from challenging authority."
Key point:
- Obedience can be reinforced by social expectations and fear of consequences.
- Establishing safe procedures for questioning instructions can reduce harmful compliance.
38. Q: How does psychological distance from a victim influence obedience?
A: Show answer
People may be more willing to inflict harm when they are psychologically or physically distant from the person affected. Milgram's research found lower obedience when participants were required to force the learner's hand onto a shock plate than when they did not need to see or touch the learner. Direct contact made the consequences of the action more immediate and personal. Greater distance can make it easier to focus on instructions while paying less attention to the suffering experienced by another person. This principle has broader implications for military operations, institutional decision-making, and situations where harmful consequences are separated from the individuals responsible for taking action.

Study instruction:
- "Explain how visibility, physical contact, and awareness of consequences influence willingness to obey harmful instructions."
Key point:
- Greater psychological distance can make harmful actions easier to perform.
- Direct contact with affected individuals can increase awareness of consequences.
39. Q: How does belief in a worthy cause influence obedience?
A: Show answer
Individuals may become more willing to obey instructions that appear to serve a legitimate, important, or socially valuable objective. In Milgram's experiment, participants believed that they were contributing to scientific research. The association with a prestigious university helped reinforce the perceived legitimacy of the activity. When the experiment was repeated without the associations of a prestigious university, obedience rates declined. This suggests that people may be influenced not only by the authority figure but also by the perceived legitimacy of the institution and the purpose of the activity. However, a worthy stated objective does not automatically justify harmful or unethical actions.

Study instruction:
- "Assess how institutional reputation, perceived legitimacy, and the apparent purpose of an activity can influence obedience."
Key point:
- A credible institution or apparently worthy purpose can strengthen obedience.
- Ethical responsibility remains necessary even when an objective appears legitimate.
40. Q: What is prejudice, and how does it influence relationships between social groups?
A: Show answer
Prejudice is a relatively enduring attitude, usually negative, directed towards members of a social group. It involves extending generalized judgments about a group to individuals simply because they belong to that group. Prejudice can influence how people interpret behaviour, distribute opportunities, communicate, and make decisions about others. It can contribute to social exclusion, discrimination, hostility, and intergroup conflict. Prejudice may be associated with social categorization, competition for resources, group identity, personality characteristics, and environmental influences. Understanding prejudice requires examining both individual attitudes and the wider social conditions that sustain unequal treatment.

Study instruction:
- "Distinguish prejudice from stereotyping and discrimination and identify the consequences of prejudiced attitudes."
Key point:
- Prejudice involves generalized attitudes towards members of a group.
- Prejudice can influence individual decisions and intergroup relationships.
41. Q: What is stereotyping, and how does it contribute to prejudice?
A: Show answer
Stereotyping is the process of categorizing individuals according to perceived group characteristics and attributing supposedly typical qualities of that group to particular individuals. Categories may involve age, race, sex, occupation, or other social characteristics. Stereotypes can become problematic when they are overly generalized, rigid, or inaccurate. For example, assuming that every member of an occupational group has the same abilities ignores individual differences. Stereotyping contributes to prejudice because group-based assumptions influence how individuals interpret and evaluate other people before considering their actual characteristics, behaviour, or circumstances.

Study instruction:
- "Identify stereotypes in everyday situations and explain how generalizations can distort judgments about individuals."
Key point:
- Stereotyping involves applying perceived group characteristics to individuals.
- Individual assessment is necessary to avoid unfair generalizations.
42. Q: What is the difference between prejudice, stereotyping, and discrimination?
A: Show answer
These concepts are related but describe different aspects of intergroup behaviour. Stereotyping involves generalized beliefs about the characteristics of a group. Prejudice involves attitudes or evaluations directed towards that group, commonly negative. Discrimination involves behaviour or actions that disadvantage individuals because of their group membership. For example, believing that a particular group is less capable is a stereotype; holding a negative attitude towards its members is prejudice; refusing qualified applicants from that group is discrimination. These processes can reinforce one another, although they do not necessarily occur together in every situation.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare three separate examples illustrating stereotyping as belief, prejudice as attitude, and discrimination as behaviour."
Key point:
- Stereotypes concern generalized beliefs.
- Prejudice concerns attitudes, while discrimination concerns actions.
43. Q: Why are stereotypes often inaccurate or misleading?
A: Show answer
Stereotypes can be misleading because they treat group-level assumptions as though they apply equally to every individual. Even when a statistical difference exists between groups, it does not establish that every member possesses the same characteristic. Stereotypes may also exaggerate differences, ignore variation within groups, and rely on unsupported assumptions. Some stereotypes are completely inaccurate, while others may contain limited information that becomes distorted through overgeneralization. Accurate individual assessment requires examining relevant evidence rather than relying on broad group classifications.

Study instruction:
- "Practice identifying overgeneralization, rigidity, and inaccurate assumptions in examples of stereotyping."
Key point:
- Group averages cannot automatically predict individual characteristics.
- Stereotypes can distort judgments and reinforce unfair treatment.
44. Q: What did the blue-eyes and brown-eyes exercise demonstrate about prejudice?
A: Show answer
Jane Elliott conducted an educational exercise with eight-year-old children to demonstrate how arbitrary group distinctions and unequal treatment could influence behaviour. She told the children that brown-eyed pupils were superior to blue-eyed pupils and provided the brown-eyed group with privileges. The blue-eyed children were described negatively and required to wear identifying collars. The children began displaying changes in behaviour: the disadvantaged group showed lower self-esteem and poorer performance, while the privileged group became more critical and oppressive. When the teacher reversed the classification, the behavioural patterns also changed, although less strongly. The exercise illustrated how prejudice and unequal treatment can be socially constructed and reinforced.

Study instruction:
- "Review the group classification, unequal privileges, behavioural changes, reversal of status, and educational purpose of the exercise."
Key point:
- Arbitrary group distinctions can influence behaviour and self-perception.
- Unequal treatment can encourage prejudice and oppressive group relationships.
45. Q: How can unequal treatment create prejudice between groups?
A: Show answer
Unequal treatment can create prejudice by establishing differences in privilege, status, recognition, and access to resources. When one group is consistently treated as superior, its members may develop feelings of entitlement and negative attitudes towards disadvantaged groups. Individuals in the disadvantaged group may experience reduced confidence, exclusion, and poorer opportunities. The blue-eyes and brown-eyes exercise demonstrated how quickly these patterns can emerge when authority figures establish arbitrary distinctions and reinforce them through unequal treatment. Similar processes can occur in workplaces, schools, and communities when institutional practices systematically favour one group over another.

Study instruction:
- "Identify how unequal privileges, institutional authority, and repeated social messages can reinforce group-based prejudice."
Key point:
- Unequal treatment can create and reinforce negative intergroup attitudes.
- Institutional practices can influence individual beliefs and behaviour.
46. Q: What are ingroups and outgroups, and how do they influence social behaviour?
A: Show answer
An ingroup is a group to which an individual feels they belong, while an outgroup consists of people perceived as belonging to a different group. Group membership can influence identity, loyalty, expectations, and judgments about others. Research discussed in the chapter indicates that even arbitrary group allocation can produce favouritism towards one's own group and discrimination against another group. This suggests that intergroup bias does not always require longstanding historical or cultural differences. Group categorization itself can create psychological distinctions that influence attitudes and behaviour.

Study instruction:
- "Identify ingroups and outgroups in workplace, educational, cultural, and community settings and examine how group membership affects judgment."
Key point:
- Ingroup membership can encourage preferential treatment of one's own group.
- Outgroup categorization can contribute to unfair judgments and discrimination.
47. Q: What is ingroup favouritism, and why can it lead to discrimination?
A: Show answer
Ingroup favouritism occurs when individuals evaluate or treat members of their own group more positively than people belonging to other groups. It may involve giving preferential access to resources, trusting ingroup members more readily, or interpreting their behaviour more favourably. When combined with negative assumptions about outgroups, ingroup favouritism can contribute to discrimination. The process may occur even when group membership is based on arbitrary distinctions rather than meaningful differences in ability or behaviour. Organizations can reduce its effects through transparent recruitment, consistent performance criteria, fair resource allocation, and accountable decision-making.

Study instruction:
- "Examine how group identity can influence recruitment, promotion, resource allocation, and interpersonal judgments."
Key point:
- People may favour their own group even when group divisions are arbitrary.
- Transparent and consistent decisions help limit unfair treatment.
48. Q: How can competition for limited resources create prejudice and intergroup conflict?
A: Show answer
Competition for limited resources can create prejudice when groups believe that their interests are incompatible. Resources may include employment, funding, power, prestige, opportunities, or access to services. When one group perceives another as a threat to its position, it may develop negative attitudes and attempt to restrict the other group's opportunities. Such perceptions can increase hostility and discrimination, particularly when resources are scarce or distributed unequally. Group conflict theory helps explain why tensions may increase when groups compete for the same limited resources. Addressing the underlying competition and distribution of opportunities is therefore important in reducing intergroup hostility.

Study instruction:
- "Analyze how resource scarcity, perceived threats, unequal opportunities, and competition can produce intergroup hostility."
Key point:
- Perceived competition can increase prejudice and group conflict.
- Addressing unequal access to resources can help reduce tensions.
49. Q: What was the Robbers' Cave experiment, and what did it investigate?
A: Show answer
The Robbers' Cave experiment, conducted by Sherif and colleagues, investigated the development of group identity, competition, prejudice, and cooperation. Twenty-two boys aged 11–12 participated in a summer-camp study. Researchers divided them into two groups, allowed each group to develop its own identity, introduced competition between them, and subsequently organized cooperative activities. The study demonstrated how group divisions and competition could generate hostility and how cooperation around shared objectives could reduce conflict. It provided an important contribution to understanding realistic group conflict and intergroup relationships.

Study instruction:
- "Review the three stages of the Robbers' Cave experiment and explain what each stage demonstrated about group behaviour."
Key point:
- Group identity and competition can generate prejudice.
- Cooperation around shared objectives can reduce intergroup hostility.
50. Q: What happened during the first stage of the Robbers' Cave experiment?
A: Show answer
During the first stage, the 22 boys were allocated to two separate groups without knowing that another group existed. Each group selected its own name, developed a shared identity, and cooperated on activities. Members also established group norms and displayed their membership through clothing and other identifying symbols. This stage demonstrated how group identity can develop through shared activities, common experiences, and agreed behavioural expectations. The formation of distinct group identities established the foundation for subsequent intergroup competition.

Study instruction:
- "Explain how shared activities, group names, symbols, and behavioural expectations contribute to group identity."
Key point:
- Group identity can develop through shared experiences and cooperation.
- Group norms establish expectations for members' behaviour.
51. Q: What happened during the second stage of the Robbers' Cave experiment?
A: Show answer
During the second stage, the two groups became aware of each other and were placed in competition for prizes through a tournament. Conflict developed quickly. Members formed negative attitudes towards the opposing group, engaged in fighting, and stole or damaged possessions. The findings demonstrated that competition between groups can create hostility even when the groups were not originally in conflict. The emergence of negative attitudes illustrates how perceived threats to group interests can reinforce stereotypes, loyalty to one's own group, and antagonism towards outsiders.

Study instruction:
- "Trace how competition changed relationships between the groups and identify the behavioural signs of escalating intergroup conflict."
Key point:
- Competition can transform separate group identities into antagonistic relationships.
- Intergroup hostility can develop rapidly when groups compete for rewards.
52. Q: What happened during the third stage of the Robbers' Cave experiment?
A: Show answer
During the third stage, researchers introduced activities designed to reduce conflict between the groups. They found that simply bringing the boys together under conditions of equal-status contact was insufficient. More effective results occurred when the groups were given superordinate goals: important objectives that both groups wanted to achieve but could accomplish only through cooperation. Examples included pooling money to rent a movie and working together to free a truck. These activities encouraged practical cooperation and helped eliminate the hostility that had developed during the competitive stage.

Study instruction:
- "Explain why shared objectives requiring cooperation were more effective than contact alone in reducing conflict."
Key point:
- Equal-status contact alone may not eliminate prejudice.
- Shared goals requiring cooperation can reduce intergroup hostility.
53. Q: What are superordinate goals, and why are they effective in reducing intergroup conflict?
A: Show answer
Superordinate goals are objectives that are important to two or more groups but cannot be achieved successfully without cooperation between them. They create a shared interest that encourages members to move beyond competing group identities and work together. In the Robbers' Cave experiment, the groups cooperated to achieve objectives such as pooling resources to rent a movie and working together to free a truck. These activities reduced hostility because each group needed the other to achieve a desirable result. Superordinate goals can also be used in organizations and communities to encourage collaboration across departments, professional groups, or cultural divisions.

Study instruction:
- "Develop practical examples of shared objectives that require cooperation between groups with previously conflicting interests."
Key point:
- Superordinate goals create shared interests across group boundaries.
- Mutual dependence can encourage cooperation and reduce prejudice.
54. Q: Why is contact alone sometimes insufficient to eliminate prejudice?
A: Show answer
Contact between different groups does not automatically reduce prejudice because interaction may reinforce existing stereotypes or reproduce unequal relationships. If members of one group remain dominant while another group has less status, authority, or opportunity, contact may strengthen rather than weaken existing divisions. Similarly, interaction organized around competition can increase hostility. The chapter explains that effective contact should involve equality, cooperation, common objectives, and opportunities for meaningful relationships. The Robbers' Cave experiment demonstrated that equal-status contact alone was insufficient, whereas cooperative activities involving superordinate goals were substantially more effective.

Study instruction:
- "Compare simple intergroup contact with structured contact involving equal status and shared objectives."
Key point:
- Contact alone does not guarantee prejudice reduction.
- The quality and structure of contact are essential.
55. Q: How can equal-status contact help reduce prejudice?
A: Show answer
Equal-status contact involves interaction between members of different groups under conditions in which neither group is systematically disadvantaged or treated as inferior. It can help reduce prejudice by creating opportunities for individuals to challenge inaccurate assumptions, recognize individual differences, and develop mutual understanding. Equal status is important because unequal relationships may reinforce existing stereotypes and power imbalances. Effective contact should also encourage cooperation and shared objectives rather than competition. For example, employees from different professional or cultural backgrounds may develop stronger working relationships when they participate as equally valued members of a joint project team.

Study instruction:
- "Identify the conditions necessary for equal-status interaction and explain how they can improve intergroup relationships."
Key point:
- Equal-status interaction can challenge negative stereotypes.
- Contact is more effective when accompanied by cooperation and fairness.
56. Q: What is ethnocentrism, and how can it contribute to prejudice?
A: Show answer
Ethnocentrism involves evaluating one's own group positively while viewing other groups less favourably. It can arise from the desire to maintain a positive social identity and strengthen feelings of belonging. Individuals may emphasize the achievements and desirable characteristics of their own group while interpreting the behaviour of outsiders negatively. This process can encourage ingroup favouritism and outgroup hostility. Ethnocentrism becomes particularly problematic when group loyalty is used to justify exclusion, unequal treatment, or the assumption that one's own cultural practices are inherently superior to those of other groups.

Study instruction:
- "Identify examples of ethnocentrism and distinguish healthy group identification from negative judgments about other groups."
Key point:
- Ethnocentrism involves favouring one's own group in comparison with others.
- Strong group identity can become a source of prejudice when it encourages superiority and exclusion.
57. Q: How can personality characteristics influence the development of prejudice?
A: Show answer
Prejudice is influenced by individual characteristics as well as social circumstances. The chapter identifies associations between prejudice and personality characteristics such as reduced flexibility and authoritarian tendencies. These characteristics may help explain why individuals exposed to similar circumstances develop different attitudes towards other groups. People who express one form of prejudice may also be more likely to express prejudice towards other groups. However, personality characteristics should not be treated as the only causes of prejudice. Social categorization, competition, unequal treatment, cultural expectations, and group identity also contribute to its development.

Study instruction:
- "Compare personality-based explanations of prejudice with explanations involving group identity and social competition."
Key point:
- Personality can influence susceptibility to prejudiced attitudes.
- Prejudice also develops through social and situational processes.
58. Q: What is scapegoating, and how does it contribute to intergroup hostility?
A: Show answer
Scapegoating occurs when aggression or blame is directed towards an innocent third party because the real source of frustration or conflict cannot easily be confronted. The target may be perceived by the aggressor as a convenient or legitimate person or group to blame. For example, individuals experiencing economic or organizational difficulties may unfairly blame a minority group rather than investigate the actual causes of the problem. Scapegoating can intensify prejudice, discrimination, and hostility because it replaces evidence-based investigation with generalized blame. Addressing it requires identifying the real causes of problems and holding responsible individuals accountable rather than blaming unrelated groups.

Study instruction:
- "Construct examples distinguishing genuine responsibility for a problem from the displacement of blame onto an uninvolved group."
Key point:
- Scapegoating redirects aggression towards an innocent or vulnerable target.
- Evidence-based investigation helps prevent unfair group blame.
59. Q: What is autistic hostility, and how can a lack of contact contribute to prejudice?
A: Show answer
Autistic hostility, as described in the chapter, refers to hostility arising from ignorance and lack of direct contact with another group. When individuals have limited opportunities to interact with people from different backgrounds, they may rely on assumptions and stereotypes rather than actual knowledge. They may also misunderstand the reasons behind other people's behaviour and interpret unfamiliar actions negatively. Meaningful interaction can help correct these misunderstandings by providing opportunities to learn about individual circumstances, motivations, and experiences. However, contact must be structured appropriately to avoid reinforcing existing inequalities or stereotypes.

Study instruction:
- "Explain how limited interpersonal contact can allow stereotypes and negative interpretations to develop."
Key point:
- Lack of meaningful contact can reinforce ignorance and hostility.
- Constructive interaction can challenge inaccurate assumptions.
60. Q: How can friendship and personal relationships reduce prejudice between groups?
A: Show answer
Friendship can reduce prejudice by creating opportunities for meaningful interaction, trust, self-disclosure, and sharing of personal experiences. When individuals develop friendship across group boundaries, they gain a more detailed understanding of people who might otherwise be viewed through generalized stereotypes. Personal relationships make it harder to treat every member of another group as though they possess identical characteristics. The chapter also identifies the potential value of indirect relationships, such as having friends who themselves have friends belonging to an outgroup. These relationships can increase familiarity and encourage more positive attitudes towards other groups.

Study instruction:
- "Identify how friendship, personal disclosure, and indirect social relationships can influence intergroup attitudes."
Key point:
- Personal relationships can challenge generalized group assumptions.
- Friendship can support trust and more positive intergroup attitudes.
61. Q: Why should group identities remain visible when designing programmes to reduce prejudice?
A: Show answer
Research discussed in the chapter suggests that positive intergroup contact can be more effective when group memberships remain identifiable rather than being ignored completely. If a positive example from another group is treated as an exceptional individual who does not represent that group, the experience may fail to change wider stereotypes. Maintaining awareness of group membership allows individuals to recognize that positive characteristics and behaviours are also present among members of the group they previously viewed negatively. This helps challenge stereotypes at the group level rather than merely changing opinions about one exceptional person.

Study instruction:
- "Explain why positive examples should challenge group stereotypes rather than being dismissed as isolated exceptions."
Key point:
- Positive intergroup experiences can challenge wider stereotypes.
- Positive examples should not be treated automatically as exceptions to group expectations.
62. Q: How can automatic environmental cues influence social attitudes and behaviour?
A: Show answer
Social behaviour can be influenced by automatic reactions to environmental information, even when individuals are not consciously aware of the influence. The chapter describes research suggesting that brief exposure to words associated with hostility can lead to more negative attitudes. Such findings indicate that environmental cues may activate associations that influence subsequent judgments and responses. These effects illustrate why social behaviour cannot always be explained through deliberate reasoning or conscious decisions alone. However, automatic influences should not be interpreted as determining behaviour completely; individuals can still reflect on their judgments and regulate their responses.

Study instruction:
- "Explain how environmental cues and automatic associations can influence social judgments without conscious awareness."
Key point:
- Social attitudes can be influenced by automatic environmental processing.
- Awareness and critical reflection can help individuals evaluate their reactions.
63. Q: How can social psychology research improve workplace productivity and employee satisfaction?
A: Show answer
Social psychology provides evidence that working conditions, leadership styles, social presence, and group relationships influence productivity and employee satisfaction. Social facilitation suggests that the presence of colleagues can influence work performance. Leadership research demonstrates that participative management can support productivity and positive relationships. Research on conformity and obedience also highlights the importance of understanding how employees respond to social pressure and authority. Organizations can apply these findings by creating clear responsibilities, encouraging constructive participation, supporting independent judgment, and developing cooperative working environments.

Study instruction:
- "Apply social facilitation and leadership concepts to workplace productivity, employee engagement, and organizational relationships."
Key point:
- Workplace performance is influenced by social and organizational conditions.
- Effective leadership should balance productivity, participation, and employee relationships.
64. Q: How can organizations reduce harmful conformity and encourage independent judgment?
A: Show answer
Organizations can reduce harmful conformity by creating an environment in which employees can express disagreement without fear of retaliation. Managers should encourage alternative opinions, request evidence before accepting recommendations, and distinguish agreement from genuine consensus. Independent reviews, confidential reporting channels, and structured discussions can help identify errors that might otherwise be ignored because of group pressure. Leaders should also recognize the value of constructive dissent and ensure that junior employees can question decisions made by senior personnel. These measures help prevent group pressure from replacing professional judgment.

Study instruction:
- "Design practical safeguards that encourage independent opinions and allow employees to challenge questionable decisions."
Key point:
- Psychological safety supports constructive disagreement.
- Independent review helps reduce the risks of group conformity.
65. Q: How can organizations prevent harmful obedience to authority?
A: Show answer
Organizations should establish clear ethical standards, transparent decision-making processes, and procedures for questioning instructions that appear unlawful, unsafe, or inappropriate. Employees need to understand that professional responsibility does not disappear simply because an instruction comes from a senior manager. Reporting channels, independent oversight, segregation of duties, documented approvals, and whistleblower protections can reduce the likelihood that authority will be used to justify misconduct. Managers should also encourage staff to raise concerns and explain the reasons behind important decisions. Effective authority should support accountability rather than suppress it.

Study instruction:
- "Develop a practical framework for identifying, challenging, documenting, and escalating inappropriate instructions."
Key point:
- Authority must operate within ethical and organizational boundaries.
- Employees need safe mechanisms for questioning improper instructions.
66. Q: Why might smaller juries be less susceptible to conformity than larger juries?
A: Show answer
The chapter discusses research suggesting that juries of six or seven members may sometimes be more effective than juries of twelve because smaller groups may be less susceptible to excessive conformity pressure and domination by a few strong personalities. Larger groups can create more complex social dynamics, including unequal participation and stronger influence from dominant members. Smaller groups may make it easier for individuals to express opinions and participate in deliberation. However, jury effectiveness also depends on legal requirements, representativeness, deliberation quality, and the complexity of the case. Group size alone does not determine the fairness or accuracy of a verdict.

Study instruction:
- "Examine how group size, dominant personalities, and majority pressure can influence collective decision-making."
Key point:
- Group size can influence participation and conformity.
- Effective decisions require independent reasoning and balanced deliberation.
67. Q: How can organizations use superordinate goals to improve cooperation between departments?
A: Show answer
Organizations can establish objectives that require different departments to contribute resources, expertise, and coordinated effort. For example, finance, procurement, operations, and programme teams may jointly implement a project that requires accurate budgeting, timely purchasing, operational delivery, and financial reporting. If the objective can only be achieved through cooperation, departments have incentives to share information and resolve differences. This approach can reduce departmental rivalry and improve organizational performance. Management should ensure that responsibilities remain clear and that shared objectives do not eliminate individual accountability.

Study instruction:
- "Develop a workplace example of a superordinate goal and identify the contributions required from different departments."
Key point:
- Shared objectives can align previously competing groups.
- Cooperation should be combined with clearly defined responsibilities.
68. Q: How can prejudice-reduction programmes be designed using social psychology principles?
A: Show answer
Effective prejudice-reduction programmes should address the conditions that create and maintain negative intergroup attitudes. They should promote equal-status interaction, meaningful relationships, shared objectives, cooperation, and opportunities for personal disclosure. Programmes should also address institutional inequalities and competition for scarce resources rather than focusing exclusively on changing individual attitudes. Positive examples should challenge stereotypes without being dismissed as exceptions. Monitoring should assess changes in attitudes, actual behaviour, access to opportunities, and the quality of intergroup relationships. Sustainable improvement requires both individual learning and supportive organizational or community practices.

Study instruction:
- "Develop a prejudice-reduction programme that combines equal-status contact, cooperative objectives, meaningful relationships, and measurable outcomes."
Key point:
- Effective prejudice reduction requires more than occasional contact.
- Sustainable improvement combines personal interaction with structural fairness.
69. Q: What is the relationship between social facilitation, leadership, conformity, obedience, and prejudice?
A: Show answer
These concepts describe different mechanisms through which people influence one another. Social facilitation examines how the presence of others affects performance. Leadership examines how individuals influence the direction and behaviour of groups. Conformity examines how people adjust their attitudes or behaviour to match group norms. Obedience examines responses to authority figures. Prejudice examines attitudes towards people belonging to other groups. Together, these processes demonstrate that social behaviour is shaped by individual characteristics, group membership, authority, social expectations, and environmental circumstances. They also show that social influence can produce both beneficial cooperation and harmful behaviour.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a comparative framework explaining the definition, mechanism, and practical consequences of all five concepts."
Key point:
- Social psychology examines multiple interconnected forms of social influence.
- Understanding these mechanisms helps explain cooperation, productivity, conformity, obedience, and intergroup conflict.
70. Q: How should social psychology findings be applied without assuming that one experiment explains every real-world situation?
A: Show answer
Social psychology findings should be applied by considering the research design, experimental conditions, population, limitations, and relevance to the situation being examined. Asch's line-judgment experiment demonstrates conformity under particular group conditions, while Milgram's experiment investigates obedience within a controlled experimental arrangement. The Robbers' Cave study provides evidence about competition and cooperation in a specific summer-camp setting. These studies offer valuable explanations, but their findings should not automatically be generalized to every culture, organization, or historical circumstance. Practical application requires combining research evidence with contextual knowledge, ethical judgment, and further investigation.

Study instruction:
- "Evaluate each major experiment by identifying its research question, method, findings, limitations, and relevance to real-world behaviour."
Key point:
- Psychological experiments provide evidence about behaviour under specified conditions.
- Applying findings requires consideration of context and research limitations.
71. Q: What ethical considerations arise when studying conformity, obedience, and prejudice?
A: Show answer
Research into social influence may involve deception, psychological pressure, emotional distress, unequal treatment, or exposure to potentially harmful situations. Milgram's experiment involved participants believing that they were administering painful electric shocks, creating significant psychological pressure. Asch's experiment exposed participants to deliberate majority disagreement, while the blue-eyes/brown-eyes exercise involved children experiencing discriminatory treatment. Such research requires careful ethical evaluation, including informed consent, protection from harm, appropriate debriefing, confidentiality, and the right to withdraw. Modern researchers must balance the scientific value of understanding harmful social processes against the responsibility to protect participants.

Study instruction:
- "Identify the ethical issues associated with deception, psychological distress, unequal treatment, and research involving children."
Key point:
- Social psychology research must protect participants from unnecessary harm.
- Debriefing and informed consent are important ethical safeguards.
72. Q: What important areas of social psychology require further research?
A: Show answer
The chapter identifies several areas that require further investigation, including group dynamics, bystander intervention, crowd behaviour, impression formation, cheating and deceit, interpersonal attraction, and social networking and Internet behaviour. It also highlights antisocial behaviour, including cyberbullying, rioting, terrorism, and hooliganism, alongside prosocial behaviour such as altruism. Future research needs to improve understanding of the factors that predict, influence, and modify these behaviours. Cognitive neuroscience also provides opportunities to investigate the brain processes involved in social interaction. These subjects extend social psychology beyond traditional studies of leadership, conformity, obedience, and prejudice.

Study instruction:
- "Identify the emerging research areas and explain how each contributes to understanding modern social behaviour."
Key point:
- Social psychology covers a broad range of social and interpersonal behaviour.
- Digital interaction and antisocial behaviour create important contemporary research challenges.
73. Q: What is bystander intervention, and why is it an important area of social psychology?
A: Show answer
Bystander intervention concerns whether and how individuals respond when they witness another person experiencing an emergency, harm, or injustice. Social psychology investigates why people sometimes intervene and why they sometimes remain passive. Relevant factors include the number of other witnesses, perceived responsibility, uncertainty about the situation, fear of embarrassment, and the perceived risks of intervening. Understanding these mechanisms can help develop practical strategies for encouraging assistance, such as assigning responsibility clearly and providing accessible reporting procedures. Although the chapter identifies bystander intervention as an important research area, it does not provide a detailed account of specific experiments on the subject.

Study instruction:
- "Investigate how group presence, perceived responsibility, uncertainty, and personal risk can influence intervention decisions."
Key point:
- Witnessing an emergency does not automatically result in intervention.
- Clear responsibility and appropriate support can encourage helping behaviour.
74. Q: How can social psychology help explain antisocial and prosocial behaviour?
A: Show answer
Antisocial behaviour involves actions that harm others or undermine social relationships and collective well-being. Examples include cyberbullying, rioting, terrorism, and hooliganism. Prosocial behaviour involves actions intended to benefit others, such as altruism, cooperation, and helping behaviour. Social psychology examines how group membership, social norms, authority, environmental conditions, identity, and interpersonal relationships influence both types of behaviour. Understanding these factors can support interventions that discourage harmful conduct and encourage cooperation, responsibility, and assistance to others.

Study instruction:
- "Compare antisocial and prosocial behaviour and identify the social conditions that can encourage or discourage each."
Key point:
- Social influence can encourage both harmful and beneficial behaviour.
- Understanding social mechanisms supports more effective behavioural interventions.
75. Q: How does social psychology explain the relationship between individual responsibility and group influence?
A: Show answer
Social psychology demonstrates that individual decisions are influenced by group expectations, authority, perceived norms, social identity, and environmental conditions. These influences can explain why individuals sometimes act differently in groups than they would when alone. However, recognizing social influence does not mean that individuals have no responsibility for their actions. People remain responsible for evaluating instructions, considering consequences, and making ethical judgments. Organizations and communities should therefore establish environments that encourage accountability, independent thinking, constructive disagreement, and responsible cooperation.

Study instruction:
- "Analyze situations in which group pressure influences individual decisions and explain how personal accountability can be maintained."
Key point:
- Group influence can shape individual decisions without eliminating personal responsibility.
- Ethical judgment and accountability remain essential in social situations.
76. Q: What are the main lessons from Chapter 9 about how people influence each other?
A: Show answer
The chapter demonstrates that human behaviour is strongly influenced by social environments and relationships. Social facilitation shows that the presence of others can change performance. Leadership research explains how individuals guide group activities and how leadership can emerge from circumstances. Conformity demonstrates that people may adjust their opinions and behaviour to match group norms. Obedience reveals the influence of perceived authority, even when instructions conflict with personal judgment. Prejudice research explains how group identity, stereotypes, competition, and unequal treatment can produce intergroup hostility. The chapter also demonstrates that cooperation, equal-status interaction, shared objectives, and meaningful relationships can help reduce conflict. Collectively, these findings show that understanding social influence is essential for improving relationships, organizational performance, ethical decision-making, and cooperation across groups.

Study instruction:
- "Review the complete chapter and be able to explain each major concept, its experimental evidence, practical applications, and implications for individual and group behaviour."
Key point:
- Social environments influence individual thoughts, feelings, and actions.
- Understanding social influence can help strengthen cooperation and reduce harmful group behaviour.
